TO P I C  6 | TR AD E
Trade could resurface at COP31 as a challenge to collective agreement; talks have been requested
Trade tensions could feature at COP31 as governments weigh climate ambition against industrial competitiveness. Developing countries fear that measures such as the CBAM disadvantage

their exporters, while the EU says they prevent carbon leakage. COP30 agreed three annual dialogues on trade's role in climate cooperation (no binding outcome), creating for the first time a
formal space for trade discussions in the UNFCCC process, but The African Group and others would like it on the formal COP31 agenda.
Do trade discussions belong at COP?
Developing countries have long pushed back against climate-related trade measures such as
the EU's Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism (CBAM) and Deforestation Regulation (EUDR).

They argue such measures are protectionism disguised as climate policy and
disproportionately impact poorer countries. Developed countries view such measures as
necessary to level the playing field for domestic firms in cases where foreign competitors face
lower environment-related compliance costs relative to domestic industries, while also arguing
that COP is not the appropriate forum to discuss trade matters. Despite this, a European
Parliament briefing noted trade is a point of “exposure” for the EU at COP31.

COP30’s agreement on trade dialogue was notable, but it may not be enough
COP30 agreed three annual climate-trade dialogues at the mid-year sessions in 2026-28, with
a high-level event in 2028. The first, in Bonn (13 June), was calmer than expected but exposed
the divide: developing countries argued that climate-linked trade measures such as CBAM
raise costs and restrict market access and called for impact assessments within the UNFCCC,
while the EU defended them as responses to environmental externalities. With trade also

raised in other Bonn rooms, it is far from clear the dialogue will contain the issue in Antalya.
The African Group of negotiators has requested a Trade dialogue on the formal agenda.
China has already said it intends to use COP31 to oppose cleantech trade barriers, with its vice
minister of the Ministry of Ecology and Environment saying the event should discuss “in depth”
the “new problem of unilateralism and protectionism undermining global collective efforts” on

climate. The African Group is also pushing for trade to be on the formal agenda. Ultimately,
trade talks could prove more consequential at COP31 than in previous years given inflationary
pressures and concerns over business competitiveness, but it is hard to assess the potential
impact other than possibly causing delays in agreement. However, even if the impact is
limited, the issue could shape negotiations elsewhere outside of the COP process.
Sources: European Parliament, Carbon Brief, Reuters

### Page 13

TOP I C 7 | G EO P O L I TI CS  & MU L TI L ATER AL I S M (1  o f 2 )
Multilateralism is at a crossroads
Geopolitical fragmentation and the US exit from the Paris Agreement make consensus among nearly 200 nations harder, encouraging “coalitions of the willing” (outside of the formal COP
negotiations) to pursue more ambitious climate goals. COP31’s success or failure in securing multilateral agreement or credible voluntary pledges could shape future COPs by showing which

approaches deliver progress. The US retreat from the Paris Agreement will also continue to have far-reaching implications for climate finance. Overall, the EU and China will be expected to lead.
The consensus agreement principle under strain
The United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) provides the
multilateral framework for COP, the forum where countries negotiate collective climate
decisions. The process is anchored by the view that all countries must act on climate change,

but the extent of their efforts depends on national circumstances. This puts the onus on
developed countries to lead. The question is whether this principle still holds against a more
fragmented backdrop and transactional diplomatic context as national budgets prioritize
security and business competitiveness. With the COP process still separating developed and
developing countries by their 1992 UNFCCC classifications, developed countries are continuing

to push for wealthier developing nations such as China and Saudi Arabia to play a greater role
on finance. This has been a major point of contention at recent summits.
The US stepping back has far-reaching implications
The US skipped COP30 and left the Paris Agreement. It will formally exit the UNFCCC in
February 2027. This retreat has weighed on broader climate-finance decisions, both from a
monetary sense and through the loss of negotiating power. While cities, states and businesses

from the US continue to support COP efforts (e.g., groups like America Is All In), the federal
administration’s absence has put more pressure on the EU and China to lead.
What does an evolving process look like?
Because formal COP decisions require consensus (or lack of objection), progress often stalls or
moves slowly. COP30 saw Brazil move items where there was strong, but not unanimous,
support such as the fossil fuel roadmap, to separate streams outside the formal COP process.

Countries disappointed with the lack of stronger mitigation action also launched a separate
summit on transitioning away from fossil fuels, held this past April in Colombia. While COP is
the flagship global forum, these actions demonstrate a shift towards “coalitions of the willing”
for issues where global agreement is not possible.
Sources: EU Council, EU Parliament, Carbon Brief, UN, WRI

TO P I C  7 | G EO P O L I TI C S  &  MU L TI L ATER AL I S M (2  o f 2 )
Much will rest on the approach of the EU and China

With the US absent, the EU and China are the two actors with the greatest diplomatic and economic leverage with which to broker compromise agreements between the different factions at
COP31. This will require careful diplomacy, with their interests not fully aligned in some areas; discussions around trade and climate finance may be particularly challenging. At the same time,
both have reiterated their continued support of climate action and multilateral decision making; a successful COP outcome would back up these commitments.

### Page 15

TO P I C  8 | AI  &  C L I MATE
The Antalya AI Pledge puts data-centre power demand on the agenda
We expect AI’s climate potential and rising electricity demand to make it a prominent COP31 topic, prompting debate over COPs’ role in data-centre energy use. The Antalya AI Pledge invites
governments and companies to commit voluntarily to sustainable infrastructure and environmental transparency. Its call for zero- and low-emissions energy infrastructure leaves room for

nuclear power, prompting discussion of its role in meeting data-centre demand alongside renewables.
The Antalya AI Pledge seeks to build stakeholder support for sustainable AI buildout
Türkiye is encouraging stakeholders (inc. corporates) to commit to the pledge, asking
signatories to “declare our shared ambition to make AI part of the climate solution. This
means sustainably powered, efficiently designed, transparently measured and available to all”.

Signatories will commit to: 1) Power and build AI infrastructure and hardware sustainably, 2)
Design, manage or procure AI responsibly across its lifecycle, 3) Support assessment and
reporting of the environmental impacts of AI systems, 4) Strengthen financial support and
technical infrastructure for AI for climate action.
The AI for Clean Technologies Initiative seeks to highlight AI climate solutions
The initiative plans to develop AI-enabled pilot projects (e.g. smart energy systems) and then

link the projects with industry partners and investors, aiming to demonstrate AI’s potential to
contribute to sustainability goals. For grids, the IEA sees AI improving forecasting, congestion
management and outage response, helping operators get more from existing lines.
The dialogue here will set the stage for AI discussions at future COPs
While the pledge is voluntary, many of these themes are already beginning to appear in early-
stage regulatory demands, with transparency and disclosure receiving particular focus. There

will be questions over how effective the pledge can be given the US has disengaged from the
COP process, though California could send a strong signal if it commits to the pledge. We will
be particularly focused on how the tech industry responds. Despite the trend of greenhushing,
we expect many to send delegations to Antalya, with AI companies and investors viewing
responsible practices and community engagement as key to avoiding backlash and delays to

AI buildout. Note that responsible AI was also a hot topic at the Principles for Responsible
Investment conference this year in October.
Sources: COP31 Presidency, IEA

### Page 16

TO P I C  9 | N ATU R E &  C AR B O N  MAR K ETS
Nature and carbon markets have key roles to play in supporting climate action
Carbon credit discussions will centre on government policies to boost demand and corporate engagement, with much of the formal UN architecture now in place. If carbon markets can scale,
the benefits would be wide-ranging (e.g. supporting emissions targets and providing finance for developing countries). Credibility and greenwashing concerns remain key risks however; we

expect prices to diverge in favour of high-integrity credits. The Tropical Forest Forever Facility (TFFF) now has ~$7.8bn of sponsor capital against a $10bn end-2026 target.
Sources: Tropical Forest Forever Facility, Coalition to Grow Carbon Markets
16
Voluntary carbon market development
Türkiye has stated a focus on carbon markets at COP31, particularly on government actions to
boost demand, highlighting the potential for carbon markets to unlock more than $50bn in

additional annual finance for developing nations. The Coalition to Grow Carbon Markets
(made up of 13 governments) has announced it will unveil a “Policy Playbook” at COP31 to
support market development. While much of Article 6 (carbon credits under UN process) has
moved to delivery, countries will aim to reach agreement on the funding model for Article 6.2
at COP31. Further carbon market progress stands to have multiple benefits; it can support
developed countries in hitting climate goals while reducing immediate decarbonisation

requirements, while also providing finance and supporting ecosystems in developing ones.
Understanding the pushback
Carbon markets have seen pushback over credit integrity (including regulatory scrutiny) and
the risk that offsets delay domestic cuts. UN involvement is needed to enforce common
accounting and integrity rules, such as the Article 6 rulebook finalised at COP29 and due for
review in 2028, that stop the same reduction being claimed twice. Without that backstop,

fragmented bilateral and voluntary schemes risk eroding the trust needed to scale the market.
Update on the TFFF
The Tropical Forest Forever Facility (TFFF), launched at COP30 last year, aims for $10bn of
sponsor contributions by year-end. It pays tropical-forest countries per hectare conserved,
discounts payments for each hectare lost or degraded, and directs at least 20% to Indigenous
Peoples and local communities. Its Charter was adopted on 22 July 2026 and received a

tailwind following the re-election of Brazilian President Lula, who is a key supporter. Shortly
after Lula’s re-election, the Netherlands pledged up to $500mn to the fund, bringing the total
pledged to $7.8bn. Further financing commitments are key, with Norway’s $3bn contribution
conditional on the fund reaching at least $10bn by the end of 2026.
Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Preview  |  October 2026

TO P I C  1 0 | P AC I F I C , O C EANS  & S EA L EV EL  R I S E
Oceans will be a priority item, with Australia promising to champion Pacific interests
Australia’s ask for ceding the hosting was a Pacific pre-COP, and it has promised to continue championing Pacific interests at COP31. The Taku Pakasoa Declaration, signed at the pre-COP,

reflects Pacific priorities, calling for faster emissions cuts, more adaptation finance and financial system reform. The climate-ocean nexus will also be a focus, including investable solutions.
Championing Pacific interests is a key focus for Australia
As part of the hosting compromise, Australia co-hosted the official pre-COP with Fiji and
Tuvalu (5–8 Oct), attended by countries including Germany, the UAE and India (though some
questioned the low number of international leaders who attended). Australia has pledged to

keep championing Pacific interests as President of the Negotiations.
Taku Pakasoa Declaration reflects Pacific Priorities
Signed on 6 Oct at the pre-COP, it calls for faster emissions cuts, a substantial increase in
climate finance (particularly for adaptation in capacity-constrained countries), reform of the
international financial architecture, and for elevating the ocean-climate nexus. It also
welcomes the 35% electrification goal.
The challenges of rising sea levels are gaining increasing prominence

Sea levels in the region are rising faster than the global average, and half of Pacific Island
infrastructure lies within 500 metres of the coast. The UN General Assembly adopted the UN
Declaration on Sea Level Rise by consensus on 24 Sept; global sea levels rose nearly 6mm in
2024, the highest annual increase on record.
Oceans are key to climate stability, but more finance is needed
Pacific nations emphasise the critical role of oceans as a natural environmental regulator.

They absorb 30% of all carbon dioxide emissions (largest carbon sink) and capture 90% of the
excess heat generated by these emissions. Investor interest in sustainable ocean solutions is
growing, with 2026 YTD blue bond issuance already an annual record, yet bond issuance
supporting UN SDG 14: Life Below Water trails many other categories; scaling this up will be a
focus at COP31. A commitment to delivering “tangible, bankable ocean outcomes” at COP31 is

one of 13 points included as part of the Trabzon Ocean Call to Action unveiled by Türkiye,
while the ORRAA has unveiled a Blue Climate Finance Pathway.
Sources: Taku Pakasoa Declaration (UNFCCC), UN Declaration on Sea Level Rise

Ethiopia will host COP32 in 2027, though questions over how COP will evolve are in focus
COP has shifted from setting targets to implementation. Ethiopia will host COP32 in 2027, where we expect a focus on delivering commitments and climate finance access, reflecting African
priorities. COP33 falls to Asia-Pacific in 2028, with China reportedly considering a bid and South Korea potentially interested. COP31–33 will test delivery in a fragmented world, with a second

AP P END I X  |   COP  TER MS
Appendix A: Key COP terms used in this report
This appendix explains the main COP process terms and negotiating concepts used throughout the report. Definitions are summarised for non-specialist readers and reflect how each term is
used in this deck.
Sources: UNFCCC; COP30 and COP31 Presidency documents; and the other sources referenced in this report
19
Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Preview  |  October 2026
TERM DEFINITION

Action Agenda Voluntary, non-binding pledges and initiatives launched alongside the formal talks. It is separate from negotiated decisions.
Annex II / Non-Annex I UNFCCC country classification. Annex II covers developed economies; Non-Annex I covers most other countries, including China and India.
Article 6 Paris Agreement framework for international carbon markets. Article 6.2 covers bilateral trading of carbon credits between countries.
Article 9

Paris Agreement finance provisions. Article 9.1 covers developed
countries’ obligation to provide finance. COP30 set a two-year work
programme for 2026-27.
Baku to Belém Roadmap
to $1.3T
Roadmap to scale climate finance to $1.3tn a year by 2035 from all
sources, inclusive of the $300bn NCQG.
Bonn sessions Mid-year technical talks of the UNFCCC Subsidiary Bodies (e.g. SB64) in Bonn, Germany, which prepare the ground for COP discussions.
Brazil fossil fuel roadmap COP30 Presidency’s voluntary roadmap for transitioning away from fossil fuels, pursued outside the formal UN negotiations.

Consensus How COP decisions are taken: adopted when no party formally objects, so a single party can theoretically block or dilute an outcome.
COP / COPs Conference of the Parties: the UNFCCC’s annual decision-making summit, where nearly 200 parties negotiate climate action.
COP Presidency The host country’s team that chairs the conference. For COP31, Türkiye’s Murat Kurum is COP President and leads the Action Agenda.
TERM DEFINITION
Final COP text Decisions adopted by consensus at the close of the COP, setting its formal outcome.

Formal agenda Negotiated items on the official COP agenda, decided by consensus. Separate from the voluntary Action Agenda.
Global Goal on
Adaptation (GGA)
Paris Agreement goal to strengthen resilience. COP30 adopted 59
indicators; the link to finance is contested.
Global Stocktake (GST) Five-yearly review of progress under the Paris Agreement. The second runs from COP31 to COP33 (2028).
Just transition
mechanism
Belém Action Mechanism agreed at COP30, focused on fairness in the

shift to a low-carbon economy.
Loss and damage fund Fund set up at COP27 and operationalised in 2023 to support vulnerable developing countries.
NCQG New Collective Quantified Goal (COP29): at least $300bn a year for developing countries by 2035, within a $1.3tn ambition.
NDC Nationally determined contribution: each country’s national climate plan, with emissions targets and actions, updated every five years.
Negotiating groups Blocs that coordinate national positions, such as the G77 & China, the EU, LDCs and AOSIS.

Paris Agreement 2015 treaty to hold warming well below 2°C and pursue 1.5°C, with national climate plans (NDCs) as its core mechanism.
Pre-COP Ministerial meeting ahead of the COP to test positions and build consensus. For COP31, Australia co-hosted a Pacific pre-COP.
President of Negotiations COP31 role held by Australia’s Chris Bowen, who drafts final texts and steers government-to-government talks.
UNFCCC UN Framework Convention on Climate Change: the 1992 treaty behind the climate process. Its parties meet annually at the COP.

## Document 346: GLOBAL MARKETS CALL_ Air Coming Out Of AI Bubble

Source PDF: all report pdf/GLOBAL MARKETS CALL_ Air Coming Out Of AI Bubble.pdf
Report source: researchmail轉寄
Date: 2026-11-08
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: GLOBAL MARKETS CALL: Air Coming Out Of AI Bubble
Date:    1 hour ago
GLOBAL MARKETS CALL: Air Coming Out Of AI Bubble

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
GLOBAL MARKETS CALL: Air
Coming Out Of AI Bubble
By Ed Yardeni & Toby Hearst • 8 Nov 2026
View in browser
Photo by Gaël Gaborel - OrbisTerrae / Unsplash

### Page 2

The AI selloff went global last week. In the US, Democrats swept
Congress in the midterm elections on Tuesday, November 3, surfing on a
huge wave of voter hostility toward new data centers. On Thursday,
investors dumped the AI trade worldwide, and Asia’s chipmakers took the
brunt of the wipeout. Overseas earnings are still climbing, but valuations
keep falling. We see the AI backlash as a speed bump, not a roadblock, at
home and abroad. Here’s more:

I. Stock Market Performance
Global sector leadership flipped this week according to the FTSE indexes
(chart). Defensive sectors held up best, with Real Estate up 2.2% and
Consumer Staples up 0.7%, while Consumer Discretionary led with a
2.7% gain. Technology (-6.7%) and Telecommunications (-7.4%) were
near the bottom. Energy was the worst performer, down 10.4%, as fears
of a post-midterm re-escalation in the Middle East faded. The FTSE World
index fell 2.5%.

The US has been gaining ground since we lowered our "Go Global"
investment strategy to market weight on October 18. The US MSCI’s
share of the All Country World MSCI’s market cap has climbed to 65.3%,
up from 62.4% in March (chart). The gains have come at the expense of
everyone else. The Emerging Markets MSCI’s share has dropped to
11.1% from 12.1% in mid-October.

### Page 3

The AI-heavy markets were at the bottom of the country ETF rankings this
week (chart). Taiwan fell 11.7% in dollars, South Korea 10.4%, and Japan
5.0%. This year’s laggards caught bids. South Africa led with an 8.5%
gain, followed by India at 6.6%. The US ETF fell 2.7%, slightly worse than
the All Country World ex-US ETF’s 1.8% decline.
Both the US and overseas ETFs have pulled back from their recent highs
(chart). Both remain in solid uptrends that began in late 2022.

### Page 4

II. Stock Market Earnings & Valuation
The All Country World ex-US MSCI’s forward earnings rose to yet another
record high this week (chart). It has soared since 2025 after going
nowhere for most of the previous decade.
Forward earnings is up 41.0% y/y, while forward revenues is up 11.2%
(chart). During solid economic expansions, forward revenue growth tends
to hover around 5%. Today’s double-digit pace confirms an economic
boom underpinning the global bull market.

### Page 5

Valuations continue to fall even as earnings soar (chart). The US MSCI’s
forward P/E is down to 18.9. Japan’s has dropped to 14.2 from 14.7 a
week ago. The All Country World ex-US trades at 12.0. The Emerging
Markets MSCI has slipped to single digits and is at 8.6 now.
III. Bonds
Sovereign bonds continued to rally worldwide this week as investors fled
the AI trade (chart). The US 10-year Treasury yield fell to 4.86%. The UK's
yield remains the highest in the group, at 5.16%, followed by Australia’s

5.00%. France’s yield has dropped to 4.77%, back below the US yield.
Japan’s yield is down to 2.63%.

### Page 6

IV. Focus on South Korea & Taiwan
South Korea's forward earnings is still rising to record highs, though more
slowly than earlier in the year (chart). The stock market isn’t buying it. The
MSCI South Korea stock price index is down more than 45% from its
record high. Its forward P/E has collapsed to 3.4, the lowest in over 20
years. Investors are betting that analysts will have to slash their earnings
estimates.
Taiwan tells the same story, but with a more sensible valuation. Its forward

earnings is also at a record high. Its forward P/E has dropped to 14.4 from
23.2 earlier this year (chart).

### Page 7

Fundamental data suggest South Korea and Taiwan should continue to
benefit from US demand for technology hardware. US imports of selected
technologies rose to a record $61.3 billion in September (chart).
Asia supplies much of that hardware. US imports from Vietnam rose to
$28.7 billion in September, and imports from Taiwan rose to $28.4 billion
(chart). Imports from South Korea rose to $19.2 billion. A slower US data
center buildout would delay that demand, not derail it. We would view a

slower pace as more sustainable over a longer period of time.

### Page 8

clause.

## Document 347: Global Views_ Downside Growth Risks

Source PDF: all report pdf/Global Views_ Downside Growth Risks.pdf
Report source: Goldman Sachs Publishing
Date: 2026-10-26
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Jan Hatzius 
+1(212)902-0394 | jan.hatzius@gs.com 
Goldman Sachs & Co. LLC
Global Views: Downside Growth Risks
26 October 2026 | 6:45AM EDT
1. So far in 2026, global growth has been remarkably unremarkable.  Both the world 
as a whole and the G3 economies are growing in line with our estimates of potential 
GDP.  Our interpretation is that fiscal easing and the positive impulse from the AI 
boom have offset the negative impulse from the war in Iran and the lingering drag 
from higher US tariffs.

2. Our standing global GDP forecasts project more of the same in 2027, as lower 
energy prices offset fading tailwinds from fiscal policy and AI.  But the balance of 
risks is on the downside.  The most immediate concern is that, far from subsiding, 
energy prices have again climbed sharply on fears of renewed Middle East escalation.  
The prices of gasoline, diesel, jet fuel, and European/Asian natural gas are now near 
or even above their March/April highs, with likely negative effects on consumers and 
energy-importing economies more broadly.

### Page 2

3. Although we don’t expect significant fiscal policy changes in 2027, the consequences 
of large-scale deficits—coupled with AI-linked borrowing and central bank hikes—have 
started to show up more clearly in higher longer-term interest rates.  The moves have 
been mostly orderly, but the increase since early 2026 averages a sizable 100bp across 
DM yield curves.  If sustained, we estimate that this increase will subtract a cumulative 
0.6% from GDP via weakness in housing, consumer durables, and business investment.  
(Only part of the drag is incorporated in our standing forecasts because we expect bond 
yields to pull back as central banks deliver fewer hikes than priced in markets.)

Exhibit 2: Oil Price Risks Are Skewed to the Upside

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research
26 October 2026   2
Goldman Sachs Global Views

### Page 3

4. The growth impulse from the AI boom was set to peak in late 2026 or early 2027 even 
before concerns around the sustainability of the AI investment boom intensified in 
recent weeks.  Although AI investment is still rising rapidly, we expect the increase as a 
share of GDP to slow over the next year.  Alongside the fading boost from last year’s 
increase in US depreciation allowances, this will weigh on capital spending growth, 
especially in the US.  AI has also boosted US consumer spending growth via a positive 
wealth effect worth an estimated 0.4pp (or 0.25% of GDP) over the past year.  This 
impact is likely to decline over the next year, gradually if stocks perform in line with our 
strategists’ reasonably upbeat expectations but more sharply if AI-related stocks 
continue to decline.

5. Our own base case remains that AI investment will ultimately pay off in terms of higher 
productivity and economywide profits.  However, one key channel for this payoff is labor 
market disruption, which has negative near-term macroeconomic effects.  And while DM 
labor markets still look solid in aggregate, we are starting to see clearer signs that the AI 
impact is spreading beyond the technology sector.  Across nonmanufacturing industries, 
the share of US firms that have adopted AI in regular business functions now shows a 
statistically significant negative correlation with employment growth and a statistically 
significant positive correlation with unemployment.

6. The Fed’s recent signals—explicit from Williams/Jefferson and implicit from Warsh—
effectively rule out a hike on October 28.  But the FOMC still looks very likely to deliver 
another 25bp hike to 4-4.25% on December 9 because spot inflation remains too high.  
And while we continue to expect significant disinflation next year on the back of 
reduced impulses from tariffs, energy prices, and AI-related prices, the risks to our 
forecast are on the upside, most obviously in energy.  Higher inflation could result in

another hike in March, but either way we see renewed cuts in late 2027/early 2028.

7. Like the Fed, the ECB will likely hold rates steady next week and deliver a final hike (to 
2.75%) at the December meeting.  However, the turbulence in the French bond market 
poses somewhat greater downside risks to that call than in the US.  The turbulence has 
tightened financial conditions to a degree that is likely to shave ¼-½pp from annualized 
growth in coming quarters.  And if it intensifies into December, markets might perceive a 
hike as signaling insufficient focus by the ECB on the risk of Euro area fragmentation.  
While the ECB could address this issue by coupling a hike with renewed balance sheet 
expansion, the hurdle for such a move is high, in part because purchases of French 
bonds would inevitably raise concerns about moral hazard with respect to the needed 
fiscal adjustment.

### Page 6

November 5.  Consistent with our view that the UK economy is stronger than widely 
perceived, GDP growth has surprised on the upside this year and the unemployment rate 
declined to 4.7% in August on a 3-month average basis (with the volatile 1-month rate 
plunging to 4.1%).  Rising inflation and the looming household energy price reset in 
January imply a significant risk of further moves, although market pricing of 4+ hikes 
(including November) still looks excessive.  We also expect hikes in Canada and New 
Zealand this week and in Australia next week.

9. Slightly faster than expected growth in Q3 has pushed up our 2026 GDP forecast for 
China to 4.6%.  But most activity indicators are much weaker.  Both property activity and 
fixed asset investment are contracting year-on-year, and while nominal retail sales 
growth picked up to +1.1% in September, large retailers (whose sales are relatively 
better measured) are still posting substantial year-on-year declines.  With domestic 
demand rising slowly at best, sustained GDP growth in the official 4.5-5% target range 
will require ongoing steep increases in the trade surplus.  But the surplus is already at 1% 
of global GDP, and further increases will eat more noticeably into output and 
employment abroad.  Ultimately, China’s trading partners may respond by imposing 
trade barriers that weigh on Chinese exports and GDP, although the measures taken so 
far have been limited.

Exhibit 8: UK Growth Forecasts Have Drifted Higher

Source: Bloomberg, Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research
26 October 2026   6
Goldman Sachs Global Views

### Page 7

10. Our strongest global view is that central banks will deliver fewer hikes than currently 
priced.  In addition, our rates strategists flag underweight positioning, diminished 
technical risks, and risk asset pressures as reasons why the recent rally might extend.  
The market’s narrow breadth makes our equity strategists wary of momentum factor 
exposure, but they still expect the S&P 500 to resume its climb after the midterm 
election, as earnings continue to grow solidly.  Our credit strategists remain cautious on 
IG technology bonds but see scope for some near-term reversal in HY given the scale of 
the recent selloff.  Our FX strategists expect the Japanese yen to act as a hedge against 
further AI jitters, in part because such jitters would shrink US-Japan interest rate 
differentials.  And our commodity strategists recommend targeted exposures to oil 
products and European natural gas to hedge near-term escalation risks alongside 
longer-term exposures to copper, gold, and select power markets to benefit from 
structural supply/demand imbalances. 
Jan Hatzius

心物價發展，引導短天期公債利率走向。
► 儘管全球利率大幅上升，然對於整體金融環
境影響甚微，反映美股和信用市場低波動狀
態，體現實質政策利率低於緊縮利率區間與
彭博金融情勢指數變化不大，當前美國貨幣
政策與市場流動性仍偏寬鬆，倘若地緣擾動
持續造成通膨居高難下，恐進一步推升聯準

會升息預期，疊加政府財政風險與貨幣政策
透明度降，加劇長天期公債利率上行風險，
長天期債以交易為主，不建議長期持有。

► 特斯拉3Q26財報好壞參半 — 毛利率與交付
量超預 期， 營收 大 致符合 ，營業 利潤 遜預
期，最大意外是自由現金流達15億美元，高
於共識負43億，資本開支指引下調成憂慮。
► 早前公司展示下代Roadster能力，本質上是
光環車，定價高受眾少，2028年才有望投
產，盈利貢獻有限。Model 3/Y成為大眾商
品，競爭激烈，利潤率壓縮。正如傳統品牌
以賽道或參賽展示技術，「飛天」旨在展示
獨有跨界整合能力，維持品牌形象溢價。
► 至於和SpaceX的潛在合併，核心在於貫通多
頂 業 務 ， 提 高 資 本 運 用 效 率 ， 共同分擔
Terafab的開支等，現時兩家合計市值約為
3.5兆美元。這項巨型潛在交易，除了要跨過
極高表決門檻外，SpaceX具極敏感的美國國
防及NASA合作背景，而特斯拉有超過20%
業務倚重上海超級工廠，合併存在嚴重地緣
衝突。預期馬斯克將在川普離任前，加速執
行Tesla與SpaceX 合併。

► 今年9月適逢地緣擾動持續推升高油價、聯準
會重啟 升息 、財政部與企 業發債 浪潮 等影
響，形塑債市今年以來單月最差表現。檢視
2021年以來美國投資級債前十大單月最差總
報酬，2022年聯準會3月啟動升息年度就佔
一半以上，顯示利率風險仍為近5年來債市最
大風險，然檢視單月最差報酬表現未來期間
總報酬，除2022年1月和3月聯準會重啟升息
前夕與當月表現明顯較弱，多數於未來1年後
多呈現正報酬，反映隨利率走升放大孳息效
益，同時挹注債券長期持有之報酬空間。
► 今年來債券市場利率波動明顯推升信用債利
差，其 中僅 以低 信 評債利 差升幅 較顯 ，此
外，9月以來利率連動度高及AI相關產業債種
也成為利差擴大重災區，凸顯債市投資人減
輕市場不確定性的投資傾向，考量信用債市
歷經利率上行利空洗禮，信用利差同步擴大

創造收益空間，可把握債信體質仍佳企業債
鎖利良機，並透過時間複利力量強化收益。
利率波動升，僅低信評利差擴大程度相對明顯9月為美國投資級債今年最差單月報酬
用時間換空間，單月最差表現形塑未來報酬機會

### Page 8

8
► 受聯準會重啟升息影響，過去一年美國公債
殖利率曲線持續陡峭化，其中利率最大升幅
落在短天期的2至3年期間，美國核心通膨與
就業仍具韌性，聯準會將持續保持緊縮貨幣
政策步調來應對高通膨，考量美國高物價與
高利率環境短期難消，宜透過縮短債券配置
天期並進行動態調整，為因應後續利率不確
定性環境的良策。
► 為因應利率不確定性，投資人可採取債券梯
的投資配置，主要選取不同到期期間及不同
類型債劵進行組合，債券梯投資重點主要在
於，根據殖利率曲線型態及利率動態建立不
同期間債券梯，假設市場利率走升且殖利率
曲線正斜率，建立短天期債券梯除了降低利
率風險，有 短期 閒 置資金 及孳息 需求 投資
人，亦可取得穩定孳息收益。若後續投資人
有短期資金需求，可以視情況將短期債券到
期後資金回收，降低只投資單一債券須持至
到期或直接變現的流動性風險。

高利率波動持續，用策略迎戰市場擾動

態決定後續配置

短天期債券梯：假設買入2檔各3、4、5及7年到期債券，
聚焦短期穩定收益
► 債券梯是一種分散投資方式，主要選取不同到期期間及不同
類型債劵進行組合，可兼具：
1) 分散利率風險：將資金分別買進不同到期年期的債券，以分
散利率風險。
2) 分散信用風險：分散投資不同發行機構債券，不會重壓單一
債券並可分散信用違約風險。
3) 降低流動性風險：若投資人有短期資金需求，可以視情況將
短期債券到期後資金回收，降低只投資單一債券須持至到期
或直接變現之流動性風險。

### Page 9

9
股市
 美股第三季獲利年增率預估為雙位數成長，市場靜待財報結果與展望，和對AI資本支出回收影響。
考量地緣衝突、殖利率偏高、評價面壓力與鄰近美國期中選舉，短期金融市場紛擾不止，可增加防
禦股增強耐震度。長期AI、科技發展趨勢不變，逢股市拉回時可分批布局，分散配置於不同國家與
產業，降低市場整體波動，亦可以槓鈴策略(防禦+成長)穩中求利。
 歐、日股可作為分散配置重點，全球利率走揚有利銀行股。此外，地緣衝突頻傳，歐洲國防軍工仍
有投資題材。
策略風格：可增加防禦股配置，中長期看
好AI題材如科技及半導體，非AI部分關注
醫療保健生技產業
區域：日本銀行股、歐洲國防
債市
 全球公債殖利率攀升，預估今年底前主要國家仍有升息空間，殖利率偏高環境使中短天期債券提供
吸引力，逢利率走高、鎖利領息。高信評債體質較佳，具穩定現金流與還款能力，聚焦風險調整後
利差具吸引力者，包含金融、通訊及公用事業等產業。
債種：中短天期高信評債鎖利，產業如金
融、通訊及公用事業等
匯市
 歐洲政治風險上升、歐元短期弱盤，美元高檔有撐。

 日本銀行再度升息時間不定，日圓區間震盪。
美元高檔有撐
商品
 中東地緣衝突短期難解，油價維持高位但震盪加劇。
 短期美國雙率走高，使金價短期承壓，惟美歐日財政赤字壓力與去美元化，使全球央行與長期投資
人買盤依舊，提供黃金中長期穩定支撐。

### Page 18

個別客戶因年齡、投資能力、金融商品交易經驗及風險承受度不同，適合之服務及/或金融商品亦有不同，應進一步與專屬理財業務人員進行評估。投資人應徹底瞭解相關服務及金融商品之風險，並審慎評估個人財力、資產狀況、
理財需求及風險承受能力，獨立判斷並自行決定是否投資及承擔有關風險。投資任何商品皆具有風險，商品以往之績效不保證未來之投資收益，本公司不負責商品之盈虧，也不保證最低之收益。
本公司保留對本文件及其內容之著作權及其他相關權利，任何人未經本公司同意，不得翻印或作其他任何用途使用(包括但不限於傳送或轉發予其他人士等)。如有違反情事，本公司除不負任何責任外，亦將對未經同意之使用與散布
等行為究責。
基金警語
投資一定有風險，基金投資有賺有賠，申購前應詳閱公開說明書
【信託產品投資須知及風險預告：（請投資人審慎評估下列各項條文內容）】
1. 基金（包括具有定期配息之基金）投資具投資風險，此一風險可能使信託本金發生全部虧損。
2. 基金委託人須自負盈虧，本公司受託投資不保本不保息，除盡善良管理人注意義務外，投資所可能產生的本金虧損、匯率損失、或基金解散、清算、移轉、合併等風險，均由委託人承擔。

3. 本公司受託投資之基金經金管會核准或同意生效，惟不表示絕無風險。基金經理公司以往之經理績效不保證基金之最低投資收益；基金經理公司除盡善良管理人之注意義務外，不負責基金之盈虧，亦不保證最低之收益，委託人
申購前應詳閱基金公開說明書。
4. 投資前應詳閱風險預告及各該基金公開說明書，確實作好財務規劃與風險評估。本公司受託投資之基金已備有公開說明書(或其中譯本)或投資人須知【前述文件併揭露有關基金應負擔之費用(境外基金含分銷費用)】，委託人可
至本公司營業處所索取或至公開資訊觀測站、基金資訊觀測站或本公司網站下載。
5. 為保護既有投資人之權益，部份境外基金設有價格稀釋調整機制及公平市價規定，投資人申購前應詳閱基金公開說明書及投資人須知。
6. 委託人瞭解並同意受託人辦理特定金錢信託業務(即委託人委託投資基金)交易時，自交易相對人取得之報酬、費用、折讓等各項利益，得作為受託人收取之信託報酬；另委託人在持有基金期間，受託人仍持續收受經理費分成報
酬，委託人可至受託人(即本公司)網站 (https://www.kgi.com.tw/zh-tw/)查詢基金之通路報酬。其基金經理費率及其分成費率與其他通路報酬最新變動情形，亦可至該網站查詢。

7. 後收型基金在贖回時，基金公司將依持有期間長短收取不同比率之遞延申購手續費，該費用將自贖回總額中扣除；另後收型基金若有收取分銷費，則申購手續費雖可遞延收取，惟每年仍需支付0%~1.06%的分銷費（按基金日平
均淨資產價值計算），可能造成實際負擔費用增加（分銷費依各基金公司而有不同，請參考本公司後收型基金投資費用暨特約事項及各基金之投資人須知），另該費用已反映於每日基金淨值中，委託人無需額外支付。
8. 委託人之交易如經基金公司認定為短線交易者，基金公司或本公司可拒絕受理申請轉換或申購要求，並可請求支付一定比例之贖回費用或其他相關費用，費用標準均依各該基金公司之規定。
9. 委託人因不同時間進場，將有不同之投資績效，過去之績效亦不代表未來績效之保證。基金配息率不代表基金報酬率，且過去配息率不代表未來配息率；基金淨值可能因市場因素而上下波動，投資人於獲配息時，宜一併注意基
金淨值之變動。又投資新興市場可能比投資已開發國家有較大的價格波動及流動性較低的風險，委託人應慎選投資標的。
10. 部分基金可能持有衍生性商品部位，該部位可能超過基金淨資產價值之100%，並可能因而造成基金淨值高度波動及衍生其他風險。

11. 基金的配息可能由基金的收益或本金中支付。任何涉及由本金支出的部分，可能導致原始投資金額減損；且部分基金配息前未先扣除應負擔之費用。由本金支付配息之相關資料，投資人可至證券投資信託事業或總代理人之公司
網站查詢。
12. 非投資等級債券基金（本類基金主要係投資於非投資等級之高風險債券且配息來源可能為本金）（主要特色係投資前揭基金之組合基金亦同），適合能承受較高風險之『非保守型』委託人且不宜占投資組合過高的比重。由於非
投資等級債券之信用評等未達投資等級或未經信用評等，且對利率變動的敏感度甚高，可能會因利率上升、市場流動性下降，或債券發行機構違約不支付本金、利息或破產而蒙受虧損，不適合無法承擔相關風險之委託人；又如
係本國投信機構募集者，則可能投資於符合美國Rule 144A規定具有私募性質之債券，雖其投資總金額不得超過基金淨資產價值之百分之三十，然該債券具有債券發行人違約之信用風險、利率風險、流動性風險、交易對手風險
及因財務訊息揭露不完整或因價格不透明導致波動性較大之風險，投資人應審慎評估；另證券投資信託事業募集之債券型基金、平衡型基金、不動產證券化基金、以追蹤、模擬或複製標的指數表現之指數股票型基金（ETF）及

指數型基金或募集之基金投資新興市場國家債券達基金淨資產價值之百分之六十以上者，或有可能投資符合美國Rule 144A規定具有私募性質之債券，亦應留意相關風險。
13. 投資於中國之基金應遵守中華民國相關法令之規定：境外基金(不含經金管會核准向證交所申請上市或交易之境外ETF)如有投資大陸地區之有價證券者，以掛牌上市有價證券及銀行間債券市場為限，且投資前述有價證券總金額
不得超過該境外基金淨資產價值之百分之二十(境外基金機構依鼓勵境外基金深耕計畫向金管會申請並經認可者得適用鼓勵境外基金深耕計畫所列放寬前揭投資總金額上限至百分之四十之優惠措施)；證券投資信託基金雖可投資
大陸地區之有價證券，惟仍應符合信託契約、公開說明書、證券投資信託基金管理辦法等相關規定及受到基金公司QFII額度之限制。又投資人須留意中國市場之政府政策、法令、會計稅務制度、經濟與市場等變動所可能產生之
投資風險。
14. 各基金於公開（含簡式）說明書或投資人須知揭露之風險報酬等級，係依據投信投顧公會「基金風險報酬等級分類標準」而訂定，本公司亦依據投信投顧公會「基金風險報酬等級分類標準」訂定基金產品風險等級。

富管理客服專線或透過財富管理客服信箱服務，查詢申訴案件處理進度。

## Document 374: NZ labour market review chartpack, Q3 2026 (2026-11-13)

Source PDF: all report pdf/NZ labour market review chartpack, Q3 2026 (2026-11-13).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-11-13
Pages: 14
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

LABOUR MARKET CHARTPACK
4 Nov 2026

### Page 2

Westpac New Zealand Economics
2
Michael Gordon, Senior Economist | +64 9 336 5670 | michael.gordon@westpac.co.nz      Connect with us
Key points – September quarter 2026
• The September quarter surveys pointed to ongoing softness in labour market conditions. The unemployment rate rose from 5.6% to 5.7%, continuing an
almost five-year run of increases. The unemployment rate is now at its highest level since September 2015.
• There were mixed messages in terms of jobs growth: the Household Labour Force Survey reported a 0.1% fall in employment, while the employer-

focused Quarterly Employment Survey suggested a modest rise. The Monthly Employment Indicator had also pointed to a small rise in jobs.
• The fall in HLFS employment was accompanied by a fall in the participation rate. These co-movements essentially reversed the surprisingly large
increases that we saw in the June quarter, which we had suggested at the time were more likely due to sampling error in the survey rather than a
genuine surge.
• We generally recommend focusing on the unemployment rate, which is less vulnerable to sampling errors – and the message here is one of softness. Our

best interpretation is that, to the extent that jobs are growing, it’s not fast enough to absorb the growth in the population.
• While the economy has returned to growth over the last year or so, hiring activity remains weak. This may be an ongoing legacy of employment trends in
previous years, when firms were relatively reluctant to lay people off during the slowdown, which then potentially left them overstaffed even as activity
started to pick up again. It’s not clear how long this effect can persist, though.

• Wage growth remains muted. Although cost-of-living pressures have increased on workers, the existing degree of spare capacity in the labour market
doesn’t give them much leverage to negotiate larger pay increases.
• Overall, we judge these results to be weaker than the RBNZ expected, in particular the rise in the unemployment rate. This data supports the views of
those on the policy committee who were less convinced in October about the potential for second-round inflation pressures in the economy.

### Page 3

Westpac New Zealand Economics
3
Employment
• The number of people employed fell by 0.1% in the September quarter, according to the
Household Labour Force Survey (HLFS). Previous quarters were revised down a little,
reflecting downward revisions to the working-age population estimates. Annual employment
growth slowed from 0.9% to 0.7%.
• This was substantially softer than our forecast of a 0.5% rise for the quarter. Our forecast
was based on the Monthly Employment Indicator (MEI), a comprehensive measure that is

drawn from income tax data. While there are some differences in coverage for these two
measures, any significant divergence between the two is most likely to reflect sampling error
in the HLFS.
• Meanwhile, the employer-focused Quarterly Employment Survey (QES) recorded a 0.1% rise
in the number of filled jobs and a 0.6% rise in hours paid. (This survey is of course vulnerable
to sampling error as well.)
• We’re most inclined towards the MEI measure of employment, given the breadth of its

coverage. But our best interpretation is that, to the extent that jobs are growing, it’s not
yet fast enough to absorb the growth in the adult population – hence the further rise in the
unemployment rate.

### Page 4

Westpac New Zealand Economics
4
Labour force participation
• The surprising fall in HLFS employment was accompanied by a drop in the participation rate
from 70.6% to 70.4%.
• These co-movements are effectively a reversal of the surprisingly large increases that we
saw in the June quarter, which we noted at the time were suggestive of sampling error in the
survey. Any such errors largely wash out when it comes to the unemployment rate, however,
which is why we recommend focusing on this measure.

• Setting aside the usual quarterly volatility, labour force participation remains caught
between two competing forces. One is cyclical weakness, especially among younger people,
who are finding it difficult to break into a “low-fire, low-hire” labour market. The teen
unemployment rate of around 25% is a record high going back to the start of the survey
in 1986.
• The other force is rising participation among the over-60s (who also make up a growing

share of the population). Kiwis have had a growing propensity to work beyond the NZ Super
age, as there is no penalty for doing so. It’s unclear to what extent this can be considered
an increase in the pool of available workers: older workers tend to have low mobility
between jobs, and if they find themselves out of work are more likely to leave the labour
force altogether.

Michael Gordon, Senior Economist | +64 9 336 5670 | michael.gordon@westpac.co.nz      Connect with us

### Page 5

Westpac New Zealand Economics
5
Unemployment and underutilisation
• The unemployment rate rose from 5.6% to 5.7% in the September quarter. This was the
highest rate since September 2015.
• While there are other indicators of spare capacity in the labour market, we’re not convinced
that there is additional information to draw from them this time.
• The broader underutilisation measure, which includes discouraged jobseekers and part-
time workers wanting more hours, was steady at 13.8% after having risen sharply from

12.9% last quarter. This measure is both higher (by definition) and more variable than the
unemployment rate, but tells a similar story in that it’s around an 11-year high.
• There was a marked 1.6% fall in the number of hours worked (although ‘usual’ hours were
down just 0.4%). However, we suspect this was largely driven by Matariki – a floating holiday
that won’t be captured in the seasonal adjustment.
• This may also account for the 2.5% rise in part-time employment, against a 0.7% fall in

full-time employment. Some workers may have been pushed into the part-time category
(less than 30 hours a week) simply by virtue of only working four days during the
survey week.

### Page 6

Westpac New Zealand Economics
6
Hiring
• Business confidence surveys have shown a lift in hiring intentions in recent months, as firms
have shaken off the initial shock of the Iran conflict and the surge in fuel prices.
• However, those stated intentions aren’t obviously turning into reality yet. Job advertisements
were down about 1% for the September quarter in seasonally adjusted terms, and they
remain well below pre-Covid levels. Job ads will capture ‘churn’ within existing roles as well

as new job creation, but both of these would be associated with a strong labour market.
• With the working-age population growing at the same time, the result was a modest fall in
the vacancy rate, one of the RBNZ’s highest-ranked measures of labour market tightness.
• We estimate that there are around 1.9 people unemployed for every job vacancy, up from
around 1.8 people this time last year.
• Firms have told us that they were reluctant to lay off workers during the economic downturn

in 2023-24, as they were wary of the extreme labour shortages that had developed in the
previous boom years. As a result, they may find themselves still overstaffed even as demand
has started to pick up again, and haven’t needed to return to hiring yet.

Westpac New Zealand Economics
7
Population and migration
• The working-age population grew by around 1.0% over the last year, compared to a peak of
3.3% growth over 2023. Jobs growth (based on the less volatile MEI measure) is still lagging
behind population growth on an annual basis.
• The pool of potential workers is generally becoming older, with the Baby Boomers
increasingly reaching pension age and beyond, and with migrant arrivals tending to be an

older cohort than we saw pre-Covid – more in their 30s and 40s than their 20s.
• Net migration flows have started to pick up from their lows since late 2025. Departures of
New Zealanders have come down from their highs, though they remain above pre-Covid
levels, as the relative appeal of the Australian jobs market has narrowed.
• Foreign arrivals have picked up to some degree, although the growth appears to have been
mostly driven by students rather than those on work visas.

Westpac New Zealand Economics
8
Sectoral and regional trends
• Jobs growth has been relatively strong in government-connected sectors such as healthcare
and education, as well as in local and central government administration.
• Mining and utilities have seen strong growth in percentage terms, but neither of these
sectors are big employers in absolute terms.
• The construction sector is now starting to see a lift in employment, after a steep decline

over 2024-25. Residential building consents have risen strongly over the last year or so,
pointing to a growing pipeline of work for the homebuilding industry.
• Sectors that are connected to international tourism, such as transport and recreational
services, had previously seen strong employment growth, but this appears to have flattened
out in recent months.
• Employment trends have generally been more positive in the South Island than the North,

which likely reflects the greater influence of the agricultural sector – while not a large
employer in itself, farmers have benefited from high export prices and their spending has
flowed into other sectors.

### Page 9

Westpac New Zealand Economics
9
Wages
• The Labour Cost Index (LCI) rose by 0.5% in the September quarter. Annual growth
remained around the lowest since 2021.
• While the overall result was in line with our forecast, the details were a little weaker than
we expected, with less contribution from private sector wages (up 0.5%) and a surprisingly
large 0.7% increase in the public sector.
• The distribution of pay rates has returned to pre-Covid trends. Only 44% of roles saw a

pay rise over the last year; of those roles that did see an increase, the average size has
converged on the 2-3% range.
• The LCI is not a measure of what workers actually receive – it reflects the cost to businesses
for a given unit of labour – and hence shouldn’t be compared to the inflation rate. A more
suitable measure is the analytical unadjusted LCI, which includes pay rises that are related
to productivity gains. This measure slowed from 3.1% to 2.9% on an annual basis, the lowest

since March 2021.
• There was a marked pickup in the proportion of firms reporting cost-of-living adjustments as
the main reason for pay increases. However, the size of those increases varied greatly, with
many of them below the rate of CPI inflation.
• This seems to reflect a shift in the justification for pay rises, rather than an overall increase in
wage pressures – firms are more likely to cite cost-of-living adjustments because they’re less
likely to cite demand-side pressures.

Westpac New Zealand Economics
10
International comparisons
• New Zealand’s unemployment rate is the 18th lowest in the OECD (out of 38 countries). This
ranking has been broadly unchanged over the last year or so.
• Notably, New Zealand’s unemployment rate is substantially above Australia’s, whereas
historically it has been lower on average. This shift in relative job opportunities has been a
key attractor for New Zealanders over the last couple of years – as it was in the early 2010s

when the Australian economy was benefiting from a boom in mining construction.
• However, that gap between unemployment rates is no longer widening, with the Australian
jobs market starting to soften this year as the RBA has raised interest rates to rein in inflation
(which was already running too high before the Iran conflict and the surge in oil prices). We
expect Australia’s unemployment to rise further over the year ahead.
• New Zealand has the 7th highest employment rate in the OECD (down from 6th last quarter).

We tend to have high rates of labour force participation for 15-64 year olds, and particularly
high participation for the over-65s, as the lack of means testing for NZ Superannuation
means there is no financial disincentive to working beyond pension age.

## Document 409: Threats to European diesel imports_ risk of shortages ruled out for the time being, but increased risk of price rises (2026-10-01)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Threats to European diesel imports_ risk of shortages ruled out for the time being, but increased risk of price rises (2026-10-01).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 2
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Paris, 1st October 2026

EcoFlash

Threats to European diesel imports: risk of shortages ruled out for the time being, but increased 
risk of price rises

In Europe, the possibility of a US ban on diesel exports has heightened concerns about price trends for this widely used 
fuel1, which has already risen significantly since the outbreak of the conflict in Iran. It has become a major political issue 
on both sides of the Atlantic in the run-up to decisive elections.

A particularly tight diesel market due to a shortage of buffer stocks 
Following the outbreak of the conflict, diesel prices did indeed see some of the sharpest rises on the oil market due to particularly 
tight market conditions. Since the end of February, whilst the price of a barrel of crude has risen by 46%, diesel prices have soared 
by 100% in Europe and by 76% in the United States.

The factors alleviating pressure on the refined products market, and more specifically the diesel market, were more limited than in 
the case of crude oil. Shipments passing through the Strait of Hormuz (around 17% of maritime diesel trade prior to the conflict) 
have fallen by around 75%. Added to this is the halt in Russian exports due to war damage, which is depriving the diesel market of 
around 12% of its maritime exports. Furthermore, unlike with crude oil, China has not acted as a stabilising force in the diesel 
market. On the contrary, the energy crisis has led to a halt in Chinese exports of refined products (including diesel) to the rest of 
Asia from April onwards. The resumption of Chinese exports over the last two months remains fragile, given, in particular, that 
diesel stocks are at their lowest level in 15 months.

The United States has eased these tensions in the diesel market by increasing its exports of refined products (up 25% on average 
in the first half of 2026 compared with the average over the last five years). This rise in exports has required high refinery utilisation 
rate (close to maximum capacity) and, to a lesser extent, a reduction in stocks (-11% compared with the average over the last five 
years as at mid-September for middle distillates2). All these factors have contributed to tightening the US domestic market and 
pushed retail diesel prices to record levels. Road diesel prices (US average) have risen by 68% since the end of February and by 
74% year-on-year.

A structurally high level of vulnerability in Europe… 
Against this market backdrop, characterised by mounting tensions and very limited room for manoeuvre, Europe would be 
particularly hard hit by a reduction in – or even a complete halt to – US exports. According to Eurostat, imports accounted for 46% 
of European demand for diesel in the first half of 2026, with 40% of these imports coming from the United States. Europe, which

1 Diesel accounts for 17% of the primary energy mix in the EU and 7.3% in the United States. 
2 A category of refined petroleum products comprising mainly diesel and aviation fuel.

### Page 2

has lost a great deal of production capacity in recent years, is operating its refineries at near-maximum capacity. Its room for 
manoeuvre is therefore limited.

… But the risk of a shortage has been ruled out in the short term 
The risk of a shortage, however, appears limited in the short term. The situation regarding European stocks is not comfortable, but 
it is not alarming. Commercial diesel stocks in the ARA region3 are currently at historically low levels (around 12 million barrels), 
23% below their level at the end of September 2025. As for emergency stocks (EU Emergency Stocks), these are, according to the 
European Commission, at a sufficient level. Eurostat estimates this figure at 270 million barrels in June 2026 (stable from a year 
earlier), representing approximately 60 days’ worth of EU consumption. Europe imports around 0.9 mb/d of diesel from the United 
States, and emergency stocks would therefore cover around 60 days’ consumption at 4.5 mb/d.

The rise in prices could be significant 
Whatever the outcome of the conflict, diesel prices are likely to continue to rise in the short term. According to our base-case 
scenario, crude oil prices are expected to remain very high in Q4 2026 due to ongoing tensions in the Persian Gulf, whilst a return 
to normal levels of diesel exports from Russia and the Middle East appears unlikely by that time.

Among the factors likely to cause future tensions, the start of the maintenance season in Europe could affect production levels, 
whilst demand for diesel in the United States is typically high in the autumn (agricultural harvests and pre-winter storage). In a 
scenario where tensions remain low, the diesel refining margin (crack spread) could rise by 5% in Q4 compared with its level at the 
end of September 2026, before gradually falling back during 2027. An intermediate scenario (a 10% rise in the crack spread in Q4 
2026) would result in moderate constraints on US diesel exports and a drawdown of European stocks. Finally, an adverse scenario 
(a 35% rise in the crack spread in Q4 2026) would imply severe restrictions on US diesel exports and a delayed European response.

What impact will this have on inflation in the euro area? 
According to our estimates, the rise in diesel prices would exacerbate the expected peak in inflation in the euro area without altering 
its timing. In our baseline scenario for September 2026 (see our recently published EcoPerspective), headline inflation would peak 
at 4.1% y/y in January 2027 before gradually falling back towards 2% by the end of the year. The two least adverse scenarios 
would add only a limited amount to the peak: +0.1 pp in the first scenario (+5% for the crack spread in Q4 compared with the end 
of Q3) and +0.2 pp in the intermediate scenario (+10%), with inflation reaching 4.2% and 4.3% respectively in January 2027. 
However, there is an upside risk in the second scenario linked to the uncertain extent of the spillover effects. In both cases, the 
main impact would be felt through fuel prices (which quickly reflect rising market prices) and would fade over the course of 2027. 
In the case of the adverse scenario (+35%), the additional inflation would be close to 0.6 pp by early 2027, pushing inflation above 
4.6%. A shock of this magnitude would, in fact, reduce businesses’ ability to absorb rising costs, which would lead to price increases 
across a broader range of goods (food, industrial goods). Subsequently, the effect would only fade gradually.

Pascal Devaux 
Economist, Middle East, Western Balkans – Energy 
pascal.devaux@bnpparibas.com

Guillaume Derrien 
Economist, Europe, Eurozone, International Trade 
guillaume.a.derrien@bnpparibas.com

3 Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp, Europe’s main hub for the storage of raw materials.

## Document 429: US SECTORS CALL_ Information Technology, Industrials & Utilities

Source PDF: all report pdf/US SECTORS CALL_ Information Technology, Industrials & Utilities.pdf
Report source: researchmail轉寄
Date: 2026-11-08

Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: US SECTORS CALL: Information Technology, Industrials & Utilities
Date:    21 minutes ago
US SECTORS CALL: Information Technology, Industrials & Utilities

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
US SECTORS CALL: Information
Technology, Industrials &
Utilities
By Ed Yardeni & Toby Hearst • 8 Nov 2026
View in browser
Photo by Element5 Digital / Unsplash

### Page 2

Last Thursday’s selloff in the stock market was an AI selloff. Investors
dumped companies that build, wire, and power data centers, following the
Democrats' sweep of Congress amid voter hostility toward new facilities.
Stocks recovered some ground on Friday. October payrolls rose by
165,000, beating expectations of 65,000. Still, the S&P 500 ended the
week down 2.7%, and Information Technology (MW) fell 6.9% (chart).
Energy (OW) was the worst-performing sector this week, down 11.9%, for

a different reason. Oil prices tumbled on hopes of a US-Iran peace deal
following the midterm elections. We are retaining our overweight
recommendation on Energy as a hedge against re-escalation. Consumer
Discretionary (UW) and Communication Services (MW) led the week, up
2.7% and 2.3%.
The market is pricing the AI backlash as a roadblock. We see a speed
bump. Investors have already cut the multiples of the build-out’s suppliers.
Analysts have yet to cut their earnings estimates. That is the next test. We

are not changing any of our sector weights. Here’s more on Information
Technology, Industrials, and Utilities:
(1) Information Technology. The selloff hit the build-out’s hardware
suppliers the hardest. Semiconductor Equipment fell 14.5% this week,
followed by Semiconductors (-10.9%) and Communication Equipment
(-9.4%) (chart).

### Page 3

Semiconductor Equipment is the clearest case of a speed bump priced as
a roadblock. The industry’s stock price index is 27% below its 200-day
moving average (chart). Yet its forward earnings and forward profit margin
are at record highs. Its forward P/E has dropped to 21.2 from about 52.3
in June.
Analysts keep raising their estimates for Semiconductor Equipment
stocks. Collectively, they expect the industry’s earnings to grow 35.4% this
year and 51.5% next year (chart). Their consensus earnings estimate for

2027 has risen steadily all year. Semiconductor Equipment is one of only
two IT industries for which analysts expect faster growth next year than
this year.

### Page 4

Valuations are far from tech-bubble extremes. IT and Communication
Services together account for 47.6% of the S&P 500’s market
capitalization and a nearly identical 47.1% of its forward earnings (chart).
In 2000, when valuations were at tech-bubble extremes, their market-cap
share far exceeded their earnings share.
Tech's forward P/E has fallen to 17.3, and Semiconductors' forward P/E is
down to 12.0. That already discounts some earnings disappointment. A

modest cut in estimates is probably priced in. A deep one is not. That’s
why we remain at a market weight rating.
(2) Industrials. The AI-exposed industries led Industrials lower this week.
Electrical Components & Equipment fell 9.1%, and Construction

### Page 5

Machinery & Heavy Trucks fell 7.8% (chart). Passenger Airlines, Industrial
Conglomerates, and Industrial Machinery all rose.
Electrical Components shows the same disconnect as Semiconductor
Equipment. Its stock price index is 13.2% below its 200-day moving
average. Yet its forward revenues and forward earnings are at record
highs, and its forward profit margin rose to a record 18.2% (chart).
Analysts expect the industry’s earnings to grow 20.5% this year and

19.1% next year. Its forward P/E has fallen to 23.0 from over 30.
The Construction Machinery & Heavy Trucks index is trading at 26%
below its 200-day moving average, even as its forward earnings have
risen to a record high. Elsewhere, Rail Transportation is up 24.4% ytd.
Analysts have trimmed the sector’s 2027 earnings growth estimate to

### Page 6

15.8%, from 19.0% in July. That is still strong growth, and it supports our
overweight rating.
(3) Utilities. The gap between this sector’s best and worst performing
industries is enormous (chart). The Independent Power Producers &
Energy Traders industry stock price index is down 18.2% ytd, including a
7.8% drop this week. Water Utilities is up 15.2% ytd, while the Utilities
sector as a whole is up just 1.0% ytd.
IPPs sell power to data centers, and investors are pricing out that

premium before analysts do. The industry’s forward earnings is up 8.4%
ytd, while its stock price index is down 18.2% (chart). Its forward P/E has
fallen to 10.0, and its forward profit margin is at a record high.

### Page 7

Analysts are starting to follow investors’ lead. They cut their 2026 revenue
growth estimate for IPPs to 15.7% from 21.6% over the summer (chart).
But they still expect IPP earnings to grow 12.0% next year. The low
valuation suggests that investors expect more estimate cuts.
We downgraded Utilities to market weight on October 2 due to rising bond
yields. The data center premium coming out of the IPPs gives us a second
reason. We remain at a market weight rating.

clause.

### Page 8

Yardeni QuickTakes © 2026 – Unsubscribe

## Document 554: 伯恩斯坦：为什么现在绝对不能抄底英伟达？

Source PDF: all report pdf/伯恩斯坦：为什么现在绝对不能抄底英伟达？.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-11-12
Pages: 5
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

伯恩斯坦将英伟达评级下调⾄ “ 与⼤市同步 ” ，⽬标价腰斩⾄ 200 美元。尽管公司基本⾯强劲
且估值极具吸引⼒，但该⾏认为 “ 发现蟑螂时绝不⽌两只 ” ，叠加继任者空缺、潜在政府调查
及供应链承诺⽣变等重重不确定性，负⾯消息尚未⻅底，建议投资者暂勿抄底、袖⼿旁
观。
摘要：
华尔街顶级券商伯恩斯坦（ Bernstein ）将英伟达股票评级从 " 跑赢⼤市 " 下调⾄ " 与⼤市同步 " ，⽬
标价从 400 美元腰斩⾄ 200 美元。触发此次下调的，不是基本⾯恶化，⽽是⼀场席卷公司最⾼
层、仍在持续发酵的管理层丑闻。
⽬前，关于英伟达 CEO ⻩仁勋的丑闻不断蔓延，公司董事会已宣布启动独⽴调查，《华尔街⽇
报》更进⼀步报道称，公司正准备将其解职。伯恩斯坦分析师 Stacy A. Rasgon 在报告中直⾔：
在新的指控⾯前，董事会⼏乎不可能让⻩仁勋留任；即便是在位，持续曝光的⻛险同样不容忽
视。
在此背景下，伯恩斯坦认为，尽管英伟达股价已跌⾄ " 近乎荒诞的低估 " ⽔平，但仍强烈建议投资

者暂时袖⼿旁观。公司基本⾯完好，但不确定性尚未出清 —— 负⾯消息的底部在哪⾥，⽬前⽆
从判断。
丑闻持续发酵，董事会骑虎难下
事件的引爆点，是上周末曝光的⼀篇媒体报道：⻩仁勋与 Alice Huang 存在不正当关系。⻩仁勋
随即于周⽇公开承认了这段关系，英伟达董事会旋即宣布启动独⽴调查。
然⽽，事态随后迅速升级。据最新报道，⻩仁勋还与⼀名直接下属存在不当关系，据称当事⼈
为公司医疗健康业务副总裁 Kimberly Powell 。⽬前英伟达正在筹备将其扫地出⻔。
对此，伯恩斯坦判断：有些 CEO 因为远⽐这轻微的原因就被终⽌职务。在新的指控浮出⽔⾯之
后，董事会继续保留⻩仁勋的可能性极低；⽽如果董事会此时仍选择按兵不动，则意味着更多
丑闻浮现只是时间问题，届时⻛险将进⼀步叠加。
基本⾯完好，但不确定性⽆法定价
伯恩斯坦强调，此次评级下调并⾮源于对英伟达业务前景的悲观判断。从其最新预测来看，公
司数据中⼼业务的成⻓轨迹依然陡峭——2026 财年数据中⼼营收预计约 3783 亿美元， 2027 财年

进⼀步攀升⾄ 6578 亿美元， 2028 财年有望突破 9182 亿美元。整体营收预计将从 2026 财年的
4069 亿美元增⻓⾄ 2027 财年的 6903 亿美元，年增速近 70% 。每股收益⽅⾯，伯恩斯坦预计
2027/2028 财年（ FY28/29 ）平均⾮ GAAP 每股盈利为 19.04 美元，据此将⽬标价设定在 200 美
元，对应约 11 倍的远期市盈率，⼤幅低于此前 21 倍的估值倍数。
报告指出，在管理层过渡期间，公司的⽇常运营应不会受到显著⼲扰。然⽽，真正令分析师感
到棘⼿的，是继任者问题。 " 内部提拔还是外部空降？他们能找到谁来接⻩仁勋的班？ "—— 这些
问题⽬前均⽆答案，⽽任何答案的出现，都将为市场带来新的变量。
" 厨房⾥的蟑螂 " ：负⾯消息或未⻅底
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⻅闻历 搜索

### Page 2

伯恩斯坦在报告中⽤了⼀个颇为形象的⽐喻来阐述其核⼼逻辑：如果你在厨房⾥发现了两只蟑
螂，最合理的推断绝不是整个厨房⾥只有两只蟑螂。
基于这⼀判断，分析师认为，当前浮现的丑闻极可能只是冰⼭⼀⻆。未来可能接踵⽽⾄的，不
仅包括更多内部指控，还有潜在的政府调查，以及由此引发的⼀系列业务层⾯的不确定性——
包括英伟达此前在供应链上所作的⼤量战略性投资、各类兜底承诺和剩余担保的命运，均⾯临
悬念。与此同时，部分竞争对⼿的股票近⽇已因相关消息⾯出现上涨，或将继续受益。
Rasgon 在报告中坦⾔，对于这⼀判断，他⾃⼰也 " 颇为纠结 " ，完全能够理解逢低买⼊的逻辑。
但他的直觉告诉他，这⼝井的深度还没有被完全探明。正因如此，伯恩斯坦选择暂时退出，从
场外观望。

## Document 645: 国内10月物价数据解读：CPI温和回落、PPI高位续升、剪刀差走阔

Source PDF: all report pdf/国内10月物价数据解读：CPI温和回落、PPI高位续升、剪刀差走阔.pdf
Report source: 浙商期货
Date: 2026-11-12
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

国内10月物价数据解读（20261109）：CPI温和回落、PPI高位续升、剪刀差走阔
期货研究报告
发布日期：2026-11-09
数据时点：2026年10月
浙商期货有限公司期货交易咨询业务资格：证监许可[2011]1287号
撰写人：朱枭鹏｜咨询证号：Z0015158｜联系方式：0571-87219355
一、极简版速读总结
核心判断：10月CPI 同比回落、PPI 同比续创阶段新高、剪刀差走阔，能源与"AI+电子"是共同主线，上游成本端涨价更显著
CPI 同比
+0.7%(↓0.2pct)
涨幅回落
CPI 环比
持平(转平)
由涨转平
PPI 同比
+5.7%(↑0.7pct)
续创阶段新高

+0.7%(↓0.5pct)
涨幅回落
剪刀差 PPI−CPI
5.0pct(↑0.9pct)
上游成本传导不足
CPI：10月同比 +0.7%（回落0.2pct），环比持平；核心CPI同比 +0.8%（回落0.2pct）。食品（鲜菜-16.9%、猪肉-8.6%）延续拖累，能源（汽油+22.4%）与电子消

费品（存储设备+45.4%）是主要支撑。
PPI：10月同比 +5.7%（扩大0.7pct、创2022年6月以来最高），环比 +0.7%（回落0.5pct）。煤炭（+36.6%）、石油气开采（+35.0%）、石油煤炭加工
（+29.9%）与化工（+16.4%）为上游主力。
成本端抬升更显著：PPI涨幅（5.7%）明显高于CPI（0.7%），上游成本推动型涨价特征突出，剪刀差走扩至约5.0pct，上游涨价向下游消费传导仍不顺畅。
浙 商 期 货 宏 观 研 究 团 队

### Page 2

二、CPI分析
2.1 总体表现
同比 +0.7%，涨幅较上月回落0.2个百分点；环比持平（由上月上涨0.2%转为持平）。1—10月平均CPI同比 +0.9%。
环比结构：工业消费品 +0.3%（汽油 +4.2%、黄金饰品 -5.9%）；服务价格由降转涨 +0.1%（国庆出行，宾馆住宿+6.5%、飞机票+3.3%、旅行社收费
+2.4%）；食品 -1.0%（降幅走阔，鲜菜环比-5.8%、蛋类-5.2%）。
同比结构：汽油 +22.4%（拉动CPI约+0.63pct），带动能源价格涨幅由7.3%扩大至9.9%；扣除能源的工业消费品 +1.2%（数据存储设备+45.4%、平板
+29.3%、计算机+23.0%、手机+16.7%，四项合计+0.26pct）；服务 +0.7%；食品 -2.9%（降幅扩大1.2pct，拖累约-0.49pct）。

### Page 3

翘尾与新涨价：环比由涨转平、食品与黄金回落压制，新涨价动能边际减弱；同比回落主因食品降幅扩大与高基数。
2.2 结构拆分
A. 核心CPI：同比 +0.8%（回落0.2pct），处约1.0%左右的温和偏低位；环比未转负，价格中枢未失稳但内生需求动能仍偏温和，工业消费品与服务对核心形
成一定支撑。

### Page 4

B. 食品项（资讯）：食品同比 -2.9%（影响CPI约-0.49pct）。分项：鲜菜 -16.9%、猪肉 -8.6%、鲜果 -3.8%（三项合计拖累约-0.54pct）；鸡蛋 +12.3%
（涨幅回落）、牛肉+8.7%、羊肉+5.8%偏强。农业农村部周报显示猪肉批发价同比降约15%、蔬菜均价同比降超30%，供给充足、需求偏弱，食品价格延续弱
势。
C. 八大类：同比六涨一降（另有居住一降）。交通通信 +5.5% 涨幅最大（汽油拉动），食品烟酒 -1.6% 跌幅最大；其他用品及服务受黄金饰品回落影响骤降至
+1.8%；医疗保健+2.4%、教育文化娱乐+1.5%、衣着+1.1%、生活用品及服务+0.2%、居住 -0.3%。

### Page 5

2.3 CPI趋势研判
消费需求修复仍偏温和，核心CPI同比约1.0%低位运行，供强需弱格局未改。逆周期政策（超长期特别国债支持以旧换新等）边际托底，但食品价格持续弱势、
猪周期与蔬菜天气扰动仍在。综合看，CPI 处于温和偏低、上行动能有限的通道，后续关注能源价格与政策落地效果。
三、PPI分析

### Page 6

3.1 总体表现
同比 +5.7%，续创2022年6月以来阶段新高（涨幅扩大0.7pct）；环比 +0.7%，涨幅较上月回落0.5pct。1—10月平均PPI同比 +2.7%。能源相关行业上涨是
主要动力，能源价格传导效应显著。
环比看：国际能源价格上行带动石油天然气开采、煤炭采选分别上涨6.0%、2.7%，连同下游4个行业合计影响PPI环比+0.61pct、占当月涨幅八成以上；叠加产
能治理（非金属矿物制品+0.8%）与AI需求（电气机械、电子设备+0.1%）。

### Page 7

3.2 结构性驱动因素
A. 生产资料（上游）：同比 +7.4%（影响PPI+5.77pct）。采掘工业 +27.9%、原材料工业 +11.6%、加工工业 +3.5%——上游-中游剪刀差走扩，上游价格
上行斜率远大于下游。
分行业（资讯）：具体行业来看，煤炭开采+36.6%、石油和天然气开采+35.0%、石油煤炭及其他燃料加工+29.9%、化学原料+16.4%；计算机通信电子
+5.9%。9个主要上涨行业合计拉动PPI同比约6.14pct（较上月+0.64pct）。

### Page 8

B. 生活资料（下游）：同比 -0.1%（影响PPI约-0.03pct），延续弱势。食品类-1.6%、衣着类-1.3%、一般日用品类-0.7%，仅耐用消费品类 +1.4%为正（AI/
电子链条支撑）。上游涨价尚未顺畅传导至终端。
3.3 PPI趋势研判
剪刀差走扩：10月PPI同比（+5.7%）与CPI同比（+0.7%）剪刀差扩大至约5.0pct，较上月走阔0.9pct。PPI-CPI剪刀差处于历史偏高水平，反映上游成本端涨
价（能源、煤炭、有色）显著强于终端消费价格，成本传导仍不顺畅，中下游制造业利润承压。

### Page 9

前瞻判断：本月PPI上行主要由输入性（国际能源）与供给侧治理主导，呈典型"上游热、下游冷"。短期PPI同比或维持高位偏强，煤炭、原油、有色是关键变
量；若国际油价高位震荡趋缓、国内煤价回落，PPI上行斜率将趋缓。基数上，下半年同比基数偏低，对同比读数仍有支撑。
四、总结与展望

### Page 10

10月通胀格局：CPI（终端）温和回落到0.7%、PPI（生产端）续升至5.7%，"上游热、下游温、食品弱"，成本端价格上行更加显著，剪刀差走扩至约
5.0pct。共同主线为能源价格（汽油拉动CPI、煤炭/石油/化工拉动PPI）与AI算力需求（电子消费品同时支撑核心CPI与下游耐用消费品）。
核心风险：
① 国际大宗商品价格波动：国际油价已计入较高地缘与供需溢价，输入性通胀压力短期内难以缓解；若油价回落，PPI上行斜率将趋缓。
② 食品价格（猪周期/天气）：猪肉、鲜菜同比深跌持续拖累CPI，猪周期反转与蔬菜天气扰动存在不确定性，或改变食品项拖累方向。
③ 政策与需求修复节奏：逆周期政策与AI算力景气能否持续向中下游价格传导，决定PPI上游涨价能否平稳向下迁移；若传导不畅，中下游利润与终端通胀修
复将偏慢。
整体判断：短期CPI温和、PPI偏强的组合延续，PPI明显高于CPI显示成本端压力更大；通胀对货币政策暂不构成紧缩约束（核心CPI仅0.8%）。四季度关注油价
走向、猪周期拐点与政策落地效果。

认为本报告可以取代自己的判断。在决定投资前，如有需要，投资者务必向专业人士咨询并谨慎决策。

---
## Document 667: 宏观深度报告：接力与转折：居民部门需求的回归——2027年展望之中国经济

Source PDF: all report pdf/宏观深度报告：接力与转折：居民部门需求的回归——2027年展望之中国经济.pdf
Report source: 东吴证券
Date: 2026-11-12
Pages: 42
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

证券研究报告·宏观报告·宏观深度报告

东吴证券研究所

2026年11月12日

[Table_Tag]    [Table_Summary] 
◼ 核心观点：2027年中国经济运行的主线，由前期的外部需求驱动，切换
至居民部门需求的回归与经济微观结构的健康再平衡。过去一年，虽然
宏观总量在AI驱动的外需支撑下保持韧性，但内部“政策放水易、实
体灌溉难”的传导梗阻犹存，居民资产负债表与消费信心承压。展望2027
年，既有接力，也有转折，外需将在高基数与海外资本开支放缓下温和
换挡，内需将接棒，全年经济增速预计仍能达到4.6%左右。房地产延
续止跌回稳趋势，加上耐用品2-2.5年的补贴透支期步入尾声，消费率
周期迎来内生拐点；另外 PPI回落有助于收窄企业亏损面并修复微观
就业增收循环，居民部门的需求回归将是2027年最大的变化。 
◼ 一、政府部门：支出比赤字更重要 
◼ （1）2026年财政支出预算完成偏慢。2026年财政资金池充沛，但前三
季度第一本账支出进度仅完成全年的70%，创下2014年以来同期最慢
纪录，表明积极财政的落脚点必须从“上游开闸”转向“下游滴灌”，切
实解决资金无法转化为实物工作量的微观瓶颈。 
◼ （2）放水易，灌溉难，财政政策需要从继续提升赤字和债务额度上限，
转向提振财政支出增速。财政支出总量=收入总量+赤字+结转结余及调
入资金，2027年保障财政支出增速主要依靠“结转结余及调入资金”。 
◼ （3）对于财政支出驱动的基建投资而言，2027年有望实现增速转正。
一方面，2026年“超收节支”结存的约1.6万亿元潜在财政余粮将成为
2027年的资金支撑。另一方面，低基数，加上五年计划第二年缓解缺项
目问题（历史上规划第二年施工投资平均反弹4.3个百分点），基建投资
有望告别连续两年的负增长，稳步回升至2.0%左右。 
◼ 二、居民部门：私人需求的回归 
◼ （1）房地产止跌回稳的积极信号：我们此前研究的48个房地产深度下
行周期平均持续时间23.4个季度，同时83.3%的样本下跌时间不会超过
30个季度。我国自2021Q3-2026Q3已持续20个季度，正在接近拐点。
①从国际经验来看，房地产投资的调整速度决定房价下跌时间。我国地
产投资占 GDP比例从 2021年前三季度 13.4%跌至 2026年前三季度
5.0%，跌幅已超过一半，表明跟各国房地产周期相比，我国这轮周期供
给侧调整速度快且幅度大。②我国供给调整快于需求，供需加速再平衡；
③商品房待售面积来看，其同比增速在2024年5月见顶达到24.7%，
2026年8月开始同比转负，现房库存由累积转入去化。④828新政推动
预售制到现房销售，在未来两三年内会进一步放缓新增住房供给的入市
节奏，加速市场平衡。 
◼ （2）今年以来消费增速偏低，主要是商品消费增速低。从零售来看，
今年前三季度商品消费同比+1.0%、服务消费+5.0%；从支出来看，商品
消费+2.7%、服务消费+4.6%。而社零内部又分为三层，增速最高的一层
是非耐用品（不含石油），前三季度月均增长6.6%；中间层是半耐用品
和餐饮，增速在2-4%；最低层是耐用品、石油和金银珠宝，增速在-5%
到-10%。 
◼ （3）2027年有三重因素驱动消费增速回升。第一，从宏观定位来看，
消费增速=经济增速+k，k可用“服务CPI-PPI”跟踪，PPI回落意味着衡
量的消费率周期可能在2027年出现拐点，消费增速与名义GDP增速差
距将收窄。第二，伴随着房地产走向止跌回稳，消费改善的基础更加稳
固。第三，耐用品消费的拖累将明显减小，透支通常持续2-2.5年，假
如耐用品延续此前历史规律，2027年社零增速有望从1.3%升至2.3%。 
◼ 三、企业部门：出口增速是关键

### Page 2

2 / 42 
◼ 2027年，我国出口增速受高基数与海外资本开支放缓影响虽有所回落，
但仍有望维持10%左右的中高速增长，主要支撑包括：一是AI相关出
口从“价格拉动”转向“数量接棒”，中国承接中低端供给缺口，受影响
较小。二是新能源汽车虽面临退税率下调与欧盟混动车配额扰动，但依
托全产业链竞争力与较强议价权，负面冲击相对可控。三是传统消费品
受益于欧美补库需求回升而延续修复态势，促使前期K型分化收敛，实
现整体外需平稳接力。 
◼ 风险提示：（1）耐用品透支的消化节奏存在不确定性。（2）房地产市场
不及预期的风险。（3）地缘政治与全球AI产业演进构成双向扰动。

### Page 6

6 / 42

展望2027年，中国经济运行的核心主线在于三大部门动能的“接力”与“转折”。回
顾2026年，经济增长高度依赖AI产业驱动的出口超预期高增，而政府部门面临“放水
易、灌溉难”的支出梗阻，居民部门则受制于地产寻底与耐用品补贴透支。 
步入 2027年，三大部门将迎来深刻的动能切换。高基数叠加 AI资本开支增速回
落，出口增速将向10%左右的稳态区间边际放缓。对政府部门而言，提振实际支出比扩
大赤字更重要，依托跨年财政余粮与“十五五”重大项目落地，发挥托底稳盘作用。而
最重要的转折，在于居民部门需求的回归，房地产正接近止跌回稳的周期拐点，叠加PPI
回落改善劳动密集型行业就业，以及耐用品透支效应消退，居民消费与购房需求将正式
接棒外需，成为2027年中国经济中稳步改善的一部分。 
1.   政府部门：支出比赤字更重要 
放水易，灌溉难，支出增速比赤字额度更重要，财政政策的重心需要从“上游开闸”
转向“下游滴灌”。市场长期将赤字率、特别国债和专项债额度，作为衡量宏观政策积极
与否的核心指标。但近两年财政资金落地转化为实物工作量较慢，当支出无法完成预算
目标时，即使上游开闸给足额度，政策效果也有限。2025年第一本账支出预算完成度只
有96.8%，2020-2024年平均98.9%，今年前三季度预算支出进度甚至慢于2025年。因
此，对当下而言，积极财政政策未必是继续提高赤字上限，而是打断梗阻，切实提振财
政支出增速。 
1.1.   回顾2026年：财政资金额度充足，但支出偏慢 
2026年财政资金额度充足。年初预算中，赤字规模5.89万亿，用于“两重两新”
的超长期特别国债1.3万亿，专项债4.4万亿，合计11.59万亿，较2025年预算增加2300
亿。四季度又有新的资金落地，包括8000亿政策性金融工具、5500亿地方债限额，两
项工具总额度比去年多3500亿。 
尽管资金额度充足，但从实际支出来看，今年支出偏慢。这体现在三个方面，一是
收支增速和预算进度，二是实际赤字和财政脉冲，三是财政存款。 
一是从收支增速和预算进度来看，财政收入增速快、支出增速慢，完成支出预算目
标的难度较大。前三季度，第一本账（一般公共预算）收入同比增长5.1%，高于全年预
算目标2.2%，支出仅增长1.0%，低于预算目标4.4%。 
如果从预算进度来看，今年前三季度财政收入进度是2021年以来最快的，已完成
全年预算的78.1%（2022-25年均74.2%）；但支出进度是2014年以来最慢的，仅完成全
年预算的70%，2022-25年均71%、2025年70.1%。 
如果考虑到土地出让、专项债等政府性基金，即合并一二本账来看广义财政收支，

### Page 7

7 / 42 
也是收入快、支出慢。前三季度，广义财政收入同比增长1.4%，但支出同比下降3.5%。

8 / 42 
二是从赤字进度和财政脉冲双双走弱来看，今年实际财政扩张力度相对不足，对总
需求的边际拉动明显放缓。首先，月度赤字率（滚动12个月财政收支缺口占GDP比重）
衡量财政向实体经济净注入资金的绝对强度。今年以来，月度赤字率自年初高点持续回
落，广义赤字率从3月10%降至9月8.6%，同期狭义赤字率从5.6%降至4.9%，表明财
政收支缺口被动收敛，实际支出进度偏慢。其次，财政脉冲（月度赤字率同比变动）反
映财政政策力度的边际变化，直接决定财政对经济增长是形成增量托举还是边际拖累。
今年狭义和广义财政脉冲同步加速下行，并双双于二季度由正转负（截至9月分别跌至
约-1.3%和-0.7%），意味着今年财政扩张力度不及去年同期。 
三是财政资金留存相对较多。财政收入快、支出慢，其结果就是资金沉淀为财政存
款。今年前三季度，财政存款累计增加1.63万亿，高于去年的1.37万亿，也高于历史
同期。

### Page 10

1.2.   展望2027：关注支出增速而非赤字 
在我国财政体制中，决定支出预算的有三方面因素，一是当年收入，二是赤字融资，
三是结转结余及调入资金。即，财政支出总量=收入总量+赤字+结转结余及调入资金。
以2026年财政预算1为例，一般公共预算总支出约30万亿，其中约22.1万亿来自于当
年收入，包括18.2万亿税收收入和3.9万亿非税收入；2万亿来自于结转结余及调入资
金，最后约5.9万亿来自于赤字融资。 
我们对三方面资金逐一拆解分析，发现，明年收入压力可能大于今年，赤字规模小
幅增加，最大增量空间来自于2026年超收节支带来的财政余粮。 
1.2.1.   财政收入面临的压力比今年更大 
从财政收入来看，明年的财政压力会比今年更大。 
一是来自于更大的土地出让收入压力。“ 828新政”后，各地房地产市场从预售制转
向现房销售的力度明显加大，这会从两方面挤压土地出让收入。从价格效应来看，预售
制转向现房销售后，开发商回款时点从拿地后6-9个月推迟到24-36个月，房企资金占
用与财务成本上升，能承受的地价相应下调；从数量效应来看，缩表与去化不确定使房
企减少拿地、收缩战线。2026年已经是历史上土地出让收入降幅最大的一年，前三季度
同比下降27.9%。假设按前三季度增速外推全年，并且极端假设下2027年下降30%，
那么土地出让收入会从3万亿降至2.1万亿左右。 
图7：土地出让收入增速

数据来源：Wind，东吴证券研究所；注：仅考虑地方本级土地出让收入 
二是PPI带来的超收因素减退。我国现行的间接税体系对物价波动极度敏感，原因
是间接税的税基直接与物价绑定，传导途径短。物价变化主要通过增值税、附加税、企

### Page 11

11 / 42 
业所得税及资源税等四大渠道影响税收收入，这四大渠道合计占全国税收收入的近 
67%。 
PPI同比下行可能带来4600亿超收收入退潮。按照历史上几次PPI下行周期的平
均弹性来看，PPI同比每下降1个百分点，税收同比增速大致下降0.4-1.6个点，平均1.0
个点左右。如果2027年PPI中枢较2026年回落2.5个百分点，仅价格端超收因素退潮，
就将直接拖累2027年税收增速约2.5个百分点。按照2026年约18.7万亿元的税收体量
测算，这意味着价格效应带来的新增税收贡献将缩减4600亿左右。

从赤字和发债规模来看，2027年财政资金额度可能小幅增加。展望2027年，我们
预计赤字率稳定在4%左右，对应赤字规模约62400亿元，较2026年的58900亿元增加
约3500亿；用于“两重两新”的超长期特别国债1.3万亿，专项债4.4万亿，其中8000
亿用于化债，特别国债和专项债规模均与 2026年持平。但政策性金融工具可能会超过
2026年，比如从8000亿进一步增加至1万亿。相比2026年，2027年总的增量资金可
能在5000-6000亿。 
1.2.2.   财政余粮有多少能转化为明年支出？ 
三类收入中，最重要的是“结转结余及调入资金”，2026年的财政余粮充足，乐观
假设下有1.6万亿资金可以支撑2027年的支出。 
“结转结余及调入资金”是什么？一是结转结余资金。结转资金是预算事项没完成，
下年继续做，对应预算资金结转下年支出；结余资金是预算事项完成了，钱有剩余，《预
算法 》规定各级一般公共预算的结余资金，应当补充预算稳定调节基金。结转结余资
金，我们举两个例子。一是结转资金，2025年中央一般公共预算使用以前年度结转资金
安排支出183.2亿元；当年中央政府性基金预算收大于支662.51亿元，其中结转下年继
续使用588.18亿元；中央国有资本经营预算结转下年支出259.91亿元。结转资金的特
征在于专款专用、按原用途跨年使用，因此不构成可统筹的财力。二是结余资金，2025
年中央一般公共预算支出结余2602.73亿元，其中含中央预备费结余500亿元；扣除用
于平衡收支的1487.47亿元后，剩余1115.26亿元全部补充中央预算稳定调节基金2。 
二是调入资金，包括横向调入和纵向调入。横向调入是从第二三本账（政府性基金
和国有资本经营预算）调入第一本账（一般公共预算）。如2026年预算中，从第三本账
调入一般公共预算5304.88亿元。纵向调入是从预算稳定基金调入。预算稳定调节基金
是实现财政收支跨年度平衡的重要手段，2025年末中央预算稳定调节基金余额为3016.6
亿元，2026年从中央预算稳定调节基金调入一般公共预算的资金为1000亿元。除了中
央外，地方也有预算稳定调节基金。 
将2026年对比2021年，预计将有大量的结转结余及调入资金，可以用于2027年
的支出。从历史规律来看，在“超收节支”年份，当年决算实际动用的结转结余及调入资
金往往显著低于年初预算，从而为次年留出调入空间（如图10）。典型如2021年，预算
目标是一般公共预算收入增长8.1%、支出增长1.8%，调入资金及使用结转结余1.68万
亿。从决算来看，财政收入增长10.7%、支出增长1%，超收和节支同时出现，最终当年
决算调入资金及使用结转结余仅1.11万亿，较预算少动用5600亿，这最终成为2022年
财政支出的支撑。 
根据《2021年中央财政决算报告》，2021年的财政余粮大量用于2022年支出。“2021
年，中央一般公共预算支出结余1592.7亿元（含中央预备费结余100亿元），加上超收

2 国务院关于2025年中央决算的报告_中国人大网

### Page 13

13 / 42 
2020.41亿元，合计3613.11亿元，全部用于补充中央预算稳定调节基金。2021年初中
央预算稳定调节基金余额278.32亿元，加上上述补充的3613.11亿元、按规定用中央政
府性基金结转资金补充的34.79亿元，2021年末中央预算稳定调节基金余额为3926.22
亿元，2022年调入一般公共预算2765亿元后余额为1161.22亿元。” 
从图 11可以清晰看到，中央预算稳定调节基金年末余额与次年调入一般公共预算
规模呈现典型的“当年蓄水、次年放水”跨年联动特征：每当年末基金余额在超收节支推
动下脉冲式冲高（如2019年、2021年、2023年），次年调入一般公共预算的资金规模
便会随之放量，成为跨年度平滑财政支出的核心蓄水池。

图10：结转结余和调入资金的预算和决算  图11：中央预算稳定基金余额和调出

数据来源：Wind，东吴证券研究所；注：2026为预算  数据来源：Wind，东吴证券研究所；注：余额来自于 每年的预算报告，非决算数据

按照“超收+节支”的口径测算，2026 年有望为 2027 年保留约 1.6 万亿元潜在财
政余粮，高于 2021 年同口径测算的约 0.94 万亿元。具体来看，假设 2026 年全年一
般公共预算收入和支出增速分别延续前三季度的 5.1%和 1.0%，以 2025 年执行数为基
数，全年收入约为 22.71 万亿元，比年初预算的 22.07 万亿元多约 6363 亿元；全年支
出约为 29.03 万亿元，比年初预算的 30.01 万亿元少约 9831 亿元，两者合计形成约 
1.62 万亿元的财力改善空间。作为对照，2021 年最终一般公共预算收入为 20.26 万亿
元，比预算多约 4905 亿元，支出为 24.57 万亿元，比预算少约 4447 亿元，合计约 9352 
亿元。因此，在预算赤字规模保持不变、节省的财政资源留待以后年度使用的假设下，
2026 年潜在跨年财力比 2021 年多约 6800 亿元，增量主要来自支出低于预算的规模
更大。这些资源可通过减少当年储备动用、补充预算稳定调节基金及结转下年等方式，
为 2027 年财政支出提供支持。

综上，回到决定财政支出的三类资金来源来看，2027年财政账本将呈现“收入承压、
赤字微增、余粮托底”的格局。尽管预售制改革与PPI回落将加大2027当年财政收入压
力，且赤字与发债额度仅小幅扩张，但 2026年“超收节支”沉淀的约 1.6万亿元财政余
粮，将成为支撑2027年财政支出最重要的增量来源。这也表明，2027年财政政策的关
键并非表面赤字率，而是存量与跨年资金转化为实际支出的增速。进一步而言，在跨年
财力相对充裕的背景下，2027年财政资金能否顺畅转化为实物工作量，核心取决于2026
年制约基建支出的“缺项目”堵点能否打通。

1.3.   投资：缺项目问题缓解后的增速改善 
2026年，广义基建投资增速连续两年下降。2025年下降1.5%后，2026年前三季度
广义基建累计同比-6.1%，加上信息基建后（电信、广播电视和卫星传输服务，互联网和
相关服务），降幅收窄至4.2%。 
项目不足是主要约束。2026 年前三季度，本年新开工项目计划总投资额累计下降
20.5%，并且新开工项目投资额自从2024年以来已经连续3年下降，表明储备施工项目
较为紧缺。此外，前三季度本年施工项目计划总投资额累计下降5.4%，是历史首次负增
长。 
相比于项目而言，今年资金相对充足，特别是保障专项债中用于项目建设的资金要
稳定在一定比例。剔除掉特殊专项债和土地储备两个用途后，今年前10个月用于项目
建设的专项债资金约为2.3万亿，占比60.3%，高于去年全年的57.7%，止住了项目建
设资金比重连续下降的趋势。

一是缺项目问题有望缓解。2027年是“十五五”第二年，“十五五”规划所确定的
109项重大工程，在完成前期论证研究后，预计在第二年大多能够开工。参考历史来看，
过去25年的五次五年规划，其中有4次，第二年的施工项目投资额同比都在回升，平
均回升4.3个点。 
二是连续两年负增长形成的超低基数，将有助于带来2027年固定资产投资同比读
数的改善。

三是地方行政动能完成考核导向适配，投资节奏迎来秩序重构与边际修复。伴随地
方换届平稳过渡与正确政绩观的内化，2026年地方投资的审慎情绪在2027年有望逐步
缓解。但在全生命周期责任机制与债务风险硬约束的长期基调下，地方难以重走规模扩
张老路。2027年的投资好转，更多是从非正常失速向常态化弱平衡过渡，而非重回以往
由信贷与地产驱动的高增长周期。

2.   居民部门：私人需求的回归 
政府部门提振支出增速仅能为经济托底，2027年中国经济真正的向上弹性，更多
来自于居民部门需求的回归。过去几年，受房地产深度调整带来的资产缩水效应制约，
2026年还叠加耐用品补贴政策后的阶段性透支，居民部门长期是总需求中较薄弱的环
节。展望2027年，住房与消费两大私人需求支柱有望迎来共振修复。一方面，历经五
年深度调整，我国住房供给侧出清速度远超需求回落，叠加房贷贴息开辟“中央财政直
达居民”新范式，房地产正从局部筑底迈向全面止跌回稳。另一方面，地产企稳对负财富
效应的阻断，配合PPI回落提振劳动密集型就业，加上耐用品透支周期步入尾声，将共
同驱动居民消费见底回升。 
2.1.   房地产：止跌回稳 
2.1.1.   回顾：2026年的积极变化 
房地产市场在2026年出现了很多积极变化，这些变化大概率将延续至2027年。 
一是从销量来看，二手房成交增速远超前两年。前三季度二手房网签面积同比增长

10.7%，远高于2024、2025全年1.3%和2.6%的增速。按前三季度增速外推全年，合并

来看，2021-2025年9月平均比3月低0.48个点，而今年仅低0.1个点，年内房价表现
远好于季节性。

中二手房价止跌回稳（环比≥0）的城市数量持续7个月稳定在10个附近。相比之下，
2024年“924”后，这一现象的持续时间只有6个月。

2.1.2.   2027年有哪些进一步止跌回稳的信号？ 
第一，从国际经验来看，我们此前研究的48个房地产深度下行周期平均持续时间
23.4个季度，同时83.3%的样本下跌时间不会超过30个季度。我国自2021Q3-2026Q3
已持续20个季度，正在接近拐点。典型案例，如美国2006年开始的房价下跌持续22
个季度；中国香港在1981-2025年间经历三次较大幅度的房价下跌，两次持续14个季
度、一次持续23个季度。

20 / 42 
第二，住房供给调整速度决定房价下跌时间，我国供给调整较快，具备止跌回稳的
供需基础。 
（1）在房地产下行周期中，供给调整节奏比需求更重要。从需求端来看，需求=需
要+购买力，二者均具备逐步回升的内生韧性。一方面，城镇化推进、家庭规模减小与住
房折旧，使潜在居住“需要”随时间推移持续累积；另一方面，名义收入的韧性叠加房
价深度回调与按揭利率走低，驱动居民购买力加速修复。 
购买力可以看BIS开发的住房可负担性指数（Housing Affordability Index）， 衡量
一个收入处于中位数的家庭，能不能负担得起一套当地平均价格住房的按揭贷款。从国
际经验来看，住房可负担性指数在房价下跌2-3年就回到上轮周期高点，表明购买力普
遍恢复较快。 
当然，尽管如此，2027年需求端还是有积极信号，在于房贷贴息政策终于落地。这
不仅标志着房地产调控正式开辟了“中央财政直达居民端”的新范式，更在2026年房
地产市场筑底改善的基础上，为 2027年需求端打开了更具想象力的向上空间。长期以
来，稳楼市高度依赖货币降息与地方零散补贴，但前者受制于银行净息差底线，后者受
困于土地财政约束。房贷贴息以“中央财政扛大头”的方式切入，实质上搭建了一条跨
周期的居民部门定向转移支付通道。贴息工具虽处起步阶段且覆盖面相对审慎，但其政
策弹性具备极大的调整空间。展望2027年，在2026年市场实现阶段性改善的背景下，
房贷贴息政策有望进一步扩容，如放宽总价和面积等限制，为 2027年住房需求端注入
多重超预期可能。

图27：2-3年后，住房可负担能力超过前高

（2）我们用国际经验做了一个验证，发现房地产投资的调整速度决定房价下跌时
间。从国际经验来看，T之后“住宅投资占GDP的比重”从高点回落到峰值75%所需时
间越长，房价下跌时间也越长，原因是供给端调整迟缓，新增供给对价格的压制持续存
在。如下图，从 17个有完整投资数据的国际比较样本来看，住宅投资下降速度和房价
下跌时间的R2达到49.5%。 
图28：房地产投资下降速度决定房价下跌时间

数据来源：IMF，OECD，BIS，东吴证券研究所；注：T是收缩期起点，T+2为起点后
两个季度，以此类推；住宅投资这里用的是住宅投资占GDP比例 
（3）从市场结构来看，我国住房供给调整较快，具备止跌回稳的供需基础。 
①对比国际经验，我国供给侧调整更快。住宅投资占GDP比例，从国际经验来看，
中位数大致从6%跌至3%，下跌一半左右见底回升；而我国从2021年前三季度13.4%
跌至2026年前三季度5.0%，跌幅已超过一半，表明跟各国房地产周期相比，我国这轮
周期供给侧调整速度快且幅度大。 
②同时，我国供给调整快于需求，供需加速再平衡。按12个月移动平均来看，2021
年以来商品房销售跌幅53.5%，新开工面积跌幅78.9%，表明我国供给端调整快于需求
端，供需正在加快实现平衡。 
③商品房待售面积同比首次转负，是另一积极信号。从商品房待售面积来看，其同
比增速在2024年5月见顶达到24.7%，2026年8月开始同比转负，9月为-0.6%。待售
面积仅统计已竣工而未售出的现房，在建未竣工房源不计入，其增减取决于竣工规模与
现房销售速度。同比转负表明竣工形成的现房增量已小于市场消化量，库存由累积转入
去化。 
④最后，828新政推动预售制到现房销售，在未来两三年内会进一步放缓新增住房

供给的入市节奏。一是回款前移能力削弱。预售节点由各地现行的正负零、主体三分之
一或二分之一统一抬升至主体结构封顶，销售回款时点后移，项目前期自有资金占用相
应拉长。二是垫资规模抬升。新出让土地项目优先实行现房销售，销售回款须待竣工验
收后方能形成，垫资周期由预售模式下不足一年延至两年以上，同等资本可支撑的在建
规模显著收缩。三是资金调度受限。首付款与个人住房贷款全额存入监管账户、按工程
进度拨付，叠加项目主办银行制，集团层面跨项目腾挪的空间基本消失。三重机制共同
压低周转率，供给入市的节奏将进一步放缓。

综上所述，无论是从全球房地产深度调整周期的时长规律来看，还是从国内“供给
加速出清、待售库存转负”与“可负担性修复、房贷贴息落地”的供需双端信号来看，2027
年我国房地产市场进一步止跌回稳的基础已日趋扎实。更为关键的是，房地产走向止跌
回稳不仅意味着地产投资自身拖累的减轻，更将打通居民部门消费修复的核心堵点。住
房作为我国居民部门最主要的存量财富载体，一旦房价止跌的信号确立并扩散，困扰居
民多年的资产缩水预期便会实质性缓解。因此，房地产市场的筑底企稳，将为 2027年
居民消费走出低谷提供支撑，推动居民部门需求修复从“资产端企稳”传导至“消费端回
升”。

2.2.   消费：见底回升

2.2.1.   2026年的消费分层 
今年以来消费增速偏低，主要是商品消费增速低。我们从零售口径与居民支出口径
的两方面消费指标来看。零售口径，今年前三季度商品和服务零售总额同比增长2.6%，
较上年同期4.8%回落2.2个百分点；其中商品零售增长1.0%，较上年同期4.6%回落3.6
个百分点，服务零售增长5.0%，仅回落0.2个百分点。2.2个百分点的总减速几乎全部
由商品零售贡献。 
居民支出口径，今年前三季度全国居民人均消费支出增长3.6%，较上年同期4.6%
回落1.0个百分点；其中人均商品性消费支出增长2.7%，较上年同期5.0%回落2.3个百
分点，人均服务性消费支出增长4.6%，回落0.3个百分点。两套口径相互印证，商品消

费增速已降至1.0%和2.7%的低位，服务消费仍维持在5.0%和4.6%的较快区间，二者
走势持续分化。 
图33：各个口径的消费增速

数据来源：Wind，东吴证券研究所；注：2025年商品和服务零售总额是根据2026年权
重和2025年增速推算

对消费结构而言，我们参考国际经验，按照使用寿命，将社零分成耐用品（不含金
银珠宝）、半耐用品、非耐用品（不含石油）、服务消费（餐饮收入）等4类，另外将金
银珠宝、石油消费两类单列。不同类型的消费，面临的消费习惯、政策约束都完全不同，
因而表现出截然不同的增速特点。 
从社零看2026年中国消费增速明显分成三层。 
增速最高的一层是非耐用品（不含石油），前三季度月均增长6.6%。这部分商品大
多是必需消费（如食品饮料），2021年以来非耐用品增速长期稳定，波动小而增速高。 
中间层是半耐用品和餐饮，前三季度半耐用品月均增长 3.5%，餐饮收入月均增长
2%。其中，半耐用品使用寿命仅次于耐用品，因此表现出可延迟消费特点，所谓“缝缝
补补又三年”即是指半耐用品中的服装消费延迟的现象。但半耐用品又没有补贴政策带
来的刺激与透支，因而不像耐用品增速一样大起大落。而餐饮消费从 2025年下半年以
来系统性下台阶，今年大多在2%上下波动。 
最低层是耐用品、石油和金银珠宝，前三季度这三类商品月均增速分别为-9.3%、-
5.8%、-5.0%。其中，耐用品消费主要受到“以旧换新”补贴带来的基数效应和透支效应
影响；同时，耐用品在社零中的比重接近20%，对整体社零增速的影响最大。 
石油消费和金银珠宝消费都是量在收缩，但驱动力不同。石油消费量收缩，是因为

居民用车从“油车”到“电车”转型，“油车”能源计入社零，“电车”能源不计入社零，

我国黄金消费量684.623吨，同比增长0.28%。其中，黄金首饰178.612吨，同比下降
33.86%；金条及金币444.576吨，同比增长26.26%；工业及其他用金61.434吨，同比
增长1.41%。

2.2.2.   透支终有结束时：2027年消费会更乐观 
展望2027年，我们认为有三重因素驱动消费增速回升。 
第一，从宏观定位来看，2027年消费增速与名义GDP增速差距将收窄。 
以“服务CPI-PPI”衡量的消费率周期可能在2027年出现拐点。我们在11月2日
外发报告《反常识和消费不等式：谁决定消费增速？》中提出，消费增速≈经济增速+k，
其中k=居民收入占GDP比重变化+消费倾向变化=居民消费率变化。2010年以来，k≥
0是长期趋势，因而“消费增速≥经济增速”大多时候成立，仅在PPI回升周期里出现
例外。进一步，我们用“服务CPI-PPI”作为月度高频指标，跟踪k的变化，二者趋势基
本一致。2026年处于PPI回升阶段，压低了“服务CPI-PPI”与k值；而2027年PPI将步
入下行周期，“服务CPI-PPI”有望转入上升通道，带动k大概率回升。因此，消费率周
期或在2027年迎来拐点，不会再像2026年那样大幅下降，可能转为小幅下滑甚至回升。
这就意味着，2027年名义消费与GDP增速的差距将明显收窄，甚至有小概率出现消费
增速追平或赶超经济增速的情况。 
从逻辑来看，2027年中国PPI同比增速回落将通过产业链利润再分配有效带动消
费增速回升。在终端定价权偏弱的“内卷式”竞争格局下，今年工业成本上涨并没有带
动消费价格明显上涨，而是更多体现为中下游消费制造与服务企业的利润挤压。 
我们预计2027年PPI趋于回落，下游消费制造业利润率趋于改善。而这些行业相
比涨价行业，覆盖了更多就业，因而其利润率改善可以转化为薪酬增长与就业预期稳定，
进而传导至终端消费。 
当然，服务CPI若能回升，将是更重要的指标，原因是服务价格是当前消费循环中
最重要的堵点。

图38：“服务CPI-PPI”跟居民消费率变动趋势一致

数据来源：Wind，东吴证券研究所；注：2026年为前8个月数据 
第二是伴随着房地产走向止跌回稳，消费改善的基础更加稳固。 
我们的研究发现，从全球57个经济体、190次房地产下行周期的历史规律来看，
消费具有领先于房价修复的特征。这意味着房价临近拐点时，消费更易反弹。在48次
深度衰退样本中，消费拐点平均在房价下跌的第10个季度（T+10）见底，领先于房价
拐点（T+22），在能够识别消费拐点的样本中，消费领先房价的样本比例高达86.8%。 
消费领先房价改善的驱动力来自于两方面。一方面，消费本质上是受收入、就业与
政策托底驱动的流量变量，对宏观经济企稳更为敏感（跨国经验中GDP拐点平均在T+8
先于房价见底），一旦房地产止跌消除了负财富效应并稳住宏观大盘，消费改善便会先
于冗长的资产负债表与信贷扩张周期启动。二是消费品的“饱和—更新周期”短于住房，
需求更早修复。

第三是耐用品消费的拖累将明显减小。 
社零的三大短板中，耐用消费品影响最大。从体量来看，2025年限额以上口径来看，
耐用品零售额约为7.5万亿，石油及其制品约为2.3万亿，金银珠宝约为3700亿。从增
速来看，今年前三个季度耐用品同比-9.3%，降幅也远大于另外两类。 
图41：社零三大短板的体量和增速

数据来源：Wind，东吴证券研究所 
耐用品消费也是三大短板中唯一有望回升的。从影响因素来看，“油转电”是影响
石油消费的长期趋势，未来几年仍将继续；而金银珠宝消费量收缩，消费金额取决于金
价变化，波动和不确定性都较大，二者很难贡献社零增量。但耐用品不同，从历史经验
来看，补贴后的耐用品减速是有明显的回升时间的，大致在2-2.5年。 
从实际数据来看，除了汽车之外，其他的耐用消费品同比增速都已经度过短期最低
点。从年内最低增速到9月来看，家具零售同比从-10.4%收窄至-3.0%，家电音像从-19.4%
到1.9%，建筑建材从-17%到-9.2%，通讯器材从0.7%到31.1%。
